#2901. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2898. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2895. Mavericks score just 9 points in opening quarter during historic loss to Jayson Tatum, Celtics

The Dallas Mavericks got out to a horrific and historically bad start on Sunday night at TD Garden.

The Boston Celtics rolled to a 42-9 lead in the opening quarter. That marked Boston's largest lead at the end of the first period in franchise history.

It gets even worse for the Mavericks. The Celtics' 42-9 scoring margin was the best from any team in the league in any quarter since 1987, when the Los Angeles Lakers outscored the Sacramento Kings 40-4 in the first quarter, according to ESPN. It was also the third-best mark by a team in a quarter in the shot-clock era, which started back in 1954.

Remarkably, it wasn't the Mavericks' worst quarter in franchise history. They managed just two points in a quarter in a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in 1997.

On Sunday, the Mavericks made just two of their first 18 field-goal attempts in the opening quarter, and went a brutal 0 of 8 from behind the arc. Of their nine points in the opening frame, five of them came from the free-throw line.

"I didn't feel like we were turning it over early," Mavericks coach Dusty May said, via the Dallas Morning News' Mike Curtis. "I felt like we had some decent looks. We didn't convert them. I'd have to go back and look at the film for any specifics of why we weren't able to score."

And while things did get better for the Mavericks, it was far too late to make a difference. The Celtics took a 30-point lead into the locker room at halftime, and then rolled to the 131-94 victory.

Jayson Tatum led Boston with 32 points, 6 rebounds and 6 assists while shooting 11 of 18 from the field in the win. Derrick White added 16 points, and Baylor Scheierman finished with 12 points and five rebounds off the bench. Boston shot 51% from the field as a team and forced 17 turnovers.

The Celtics are now 11-2 on the season. They have won six straight, too, and hold a slight lead over the Philadelphia 76ers in the Eastern Conference standings.

Zaccharie Risacher led the Mavericks with 18 points off the bench after he went 8 of 9 from the field. Cooper Flagg finished with 17 points, and Walter Clayton added 13 points. The Mavericks were coming off a loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers in Ohio on Saturday night.

They have now lost three in a row and sit at 7-7 on the season. Thankfully, after a rough three-game road trip capped by a truly bad outing on Sunday, Flagg and the Mavericks will have three days to recover. They'll host the Golden State Warriors on Wednesday night.

#2892. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2889. Utah Jazz lose heartbreaking game against San Antonio Spurs

The Jazz welcomed the red-hot San Antonio Spurs to the Delta Center on Sunday afternoon, hoping to snap an 11-game losing streak. Unfortunately for Utah, the Spurs entered the game riding an 11-game winning streak of their own. Polar opposites. San Antonio pulled away in the final minutes, winning 110-102.

This is about as drastic of a matchup as you'll see in the NBA, but the Jazz came out hot, looking to build on the momentum they established against another one of the league's best teams, the Houston Rockets, who they narrowly lost to just a few days ago.

The Jazz put up 33 points in the first quarter, led by Mo Bamba, who scored 10. Speaking of Bamba, what an incredible performance he put together. While the Jazz ended up losing, this will forever be remembered as the Mo Bamba game.

Bamba finished with 26 points, five rebounds, four assists and three combined steals and blocks, highlighted by shooting a perfect 6-for-6 from 3-point range. The breakout game took a while for Bamba, but it finally happened.

The Jazz held a 10-point lead with just under two minutes remaining in the first quarter. The second quarter was a response from the Spurs, who eventually built a 10-point lead with nine minutes remaining in the third.

It looked as if the red-hot Spurs were going to run away with their 12th straight win.

Peterson went on to score eight straight points, making it look easy. He also could've easily earned a few trips to the free-throw line, yet he finished with zero attempts. As a team, the Jazz attempted just six free throws compared to San Antonio's 20.

It wasn't Peterson's best night, yet he still managed to demonstrate the talent that could lead him to receiving MVP votes someday. If a bad night for Peterson consists of 17 points, five rebounds, four assists and three combined steals and blocks, then goodness, everyone else is in trouble.

His efficiency was actually solid until the fourth quarter, when he simply couldn't get a shot to fall, going 0-for-5 in the final period. Several of his attempts came up short, leading me to wonder whether all the minutes Peterson has played are starting to wear him down just a little.

Peterson will be just fine, and he doesn't even turn 20 until January.

Keyonte George had another rough night, shooting under 30% from the field for the second time since returning from his ankle sprain. His shot selection was questionable at times, and that was the biggest difference between Peterson and George tonight. Neither was particularly efficient, but Peterson was generating good looks while George often found himself forcing difficult shots.

Isaiah Collier and Kyle Filipowski both received DNPs from head coach Will Hardy.

Josh Green and Josh Okogie both made positive contributions, with Green finishing at plus-4 and Okogie at plus-2. Svi Mykhailiuk, who has received plenty of criticism for his play, deserves some credit tonight after going 2-for-3 from beyond the arc and finishing with a team-high plus-15.

The Jazz are now a league-worst 1-13, having lost 12 consecutive games.

I know the injuries have been crazy. George missed the Miami game, and with a good performance from him in that matchup, Utah probably comes away with a win.

We also haven't seen projected starter Jusuf Nurkić yet, although he was going through some warmups before Sunday's game. Getting Nurkić back will help, but it won't solve everything. Don't expect him to shoot 6-for-6 from deep and score 26 points like Bamba did today.

Nobody can argue that missing two All-Star-caliber players isn't having a massive impact on this Jazz team right now. Jaren Jackson Jr. and Lauri Markkanen are clearly missed.

That being said, there's still at least a month before Markkanen returns and probably a month and a half, minimum, before Jackson is back.

This current group has to figure something out. Otherwise, when the cavalry finally arrives, it might not matter.

And don't forget, Jaxson Hayes also missed today's game.

Will Hardy has to figure things out as soon as possible. At this point, it's not even about making the playoffs or the play-in tournament. It's about showing that he's capable of winning basketball games.

Right now, that's literally something the Jazz cannot do.

If one Dejounte Murray shot goes differently, the Jazz are sitting at 0-14.

I'm not sold on Hardy right now. These weird two-man games he runs, which seemingly always result in a contested jump shot during the final seconds of the shot clock, continue to infuriate and confuse me.

On the other side, Dylan Harper dominated the Jazz in the fourth quarter. Stephon Castle mostly had his way as well, using his strength to overpower Peterson on a few occasions.

Victor Wembanyama was mostly slowed down in the second half. After posting 21 points in the first half, he managed just five in the second. For a player of his stature, you'll take that.

The Jazz wasted an incredible performance from Bamba today. They played well enough to win, but for what feels like the hundredth time this season, Utah was unable to close out a game when it mattered most.

The young Jazz will search for answers Tuesday when they take on a Los Angeles Clippers team with just two wins of its own. Tipoff is scheduled for 9 p.m. MST.

#2888. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2887. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations (PDF)

#2884. Not a cold streak; the Utah Jazz are historically bad

When the buzzer sounded on San Antonio's 110-102 win in Salt Lake City, the reigning Western Conference champions tallied their 12th consecutive win, pushing their record to an NBA-leading 12-2. The Utah Jazz, losers of Sunday's Delta Center duel, likewise extended an active streak of 12 games. 12 losses in a row.

12 straight losses are tied for the second-longest losing streak in franchise history, but that's not even the worst part of this all.

This is only the second time the Jazz have started a season 1-13. The first time? The Jazz played their home games in New Orleans, Louisiana, and were playing their very first season as an NBA franchise. That 1974-75 team opened the season with a 1-16 record and fired head coach Scotty Robertson following their 14th loss.

This Utah Jazz team isn't just bad — they're historically bad.

Yes, this year's team has been playing at a deficit when it comes to injuries. They would have likely picked up a few more wins to this point were Jaren Jackson Jr., Jusuf Nurkic, and Lauri Markkanen not seated courtside, modeling the Polo Fall 2026 line at the end of the bench. Even so, Utah's foundation is riddled with cracks and is nowhere near the level of a playoff participant.

Undersized at the wing, Utah is consistently out-physicaled and outdone athletically against every team they've faced.

It's not all bad news, though. The Jazz's stars and veterans are all expected to return in a month or so, and they have already faced each of the Western Conference's top-six teams. After facing this slate in the early season, Utah's schedule will only get easier:

Even at full strength, Utah couldn't have reasonably expected to escape that gauntlet with a winning record.

The biggest issue facing the Jazz right now isn't injuries. It isn't inexperience. It isn't even personnel. The Jazz simply don't know how to win — they've never tried before.

It's a team that has grown accustomed to losing for the last four years, and those habits have reappeared night after night.

They took San Antonio and New Orleans into overtime, only to concede a 12-0 run in each of those additional five-minute periods. They fumbled away a 10-point third-quarter lead against Houston but clawed back to within one point at the 2:46 mark, only to relinquish a 2-8 run in crunch time. Down just one point against Memphis with one minute remaining, Utah failed to score again. Behind LA by one point with three minutes to go, the Jazz still couldn't seal the deal with JJJ and Markkanen active.

They've put themself in a position to win several times, but only once did they hold on to win.

How's this for glass-half-empty: Utah is one Dejounte Murray jumper away from being 0-14.

The Jazz front office continues to back Will Hardy as head coach, determined to support the man who patiently endured four years of non-competitive basketball to lead Utah back into the playoffs when the time is right. Just as Utah's roster is learning how to win in a competitive NBA, Hardy's baptism by fire has likewise left him a bit seared.

Now is not the time to take drastic measures — still 68 games to go in the season — but Utah's issues are buried much deeper than a string of bad luck.

Calvin Barrett is the Associate Editor for SLC Dunk and a Utah Jazz columnist for the Standard-Examiner. Originally from Springville, Utah, he currently writes from his desk in Japan.

#2882. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2880. Stats Rundown: 3 numbers to know from the Mavericks’ 131-94 loss at the Boston Celtics

The Dallas Mavericks lost in embarrassing fashion, 131-94 to the Boston Celtics Sunday night in Boston. This is the Mavericks third loss in a row to drop the team back to .500 at 7-7, with all three losses being fairly dispiriting.

It started awful and never got better, with the Mavericks trailing by a double-take-inducing 33 points at the end of the first quarter. The Mavericks had zero gumption or energy to start the game, after losing a game Saturday night to a Cleveland team with zero of their starters active.

Zaccharie Risacher led the Mavericks with 18 points. Jayson Tatum led Boston and all scorers with 32, as the Celtics shot over 50 percent from the field and over 40 percent from three.

It's early, but the Mavericks have reached their first crisis point of the season — if they want to be the surprise playoff team people thought after the team started 7-4, the turnaround has to happen quickly.

The Mavericks didn't shoot the ball poorly, considering their personnel, but even with 14 made threes, the Celtics still made nine more three pointers. It was a gap too wide to overcome.

Boston is a machine, and the Mavericks couldn't keep up. Dallas' defense features a lot of forwards and bigs, but the footspeed and defensive talent on the perimeter waxes and wanes — Dallas can recover with splashy defensive plays and deflections, but the team is prone to blow-bys since the Mavericks don't have much point-of-attack defense. The Celtics got the Mavericks into rotation early and Dallas never recovered.

After a meteroic start to the season, Flagg is officially in a slump. He was 5-of-15 against the Celtics, including 2-of-6 from three. Flagg has been under 40 percent from the field in four of the eight games he's played in November, and been under 45 percent in six of the eight. He's now shooting just 42 percent in November, and 26 percent from three.

Dallas' lineup did him no favors tonight, as Dusty May rolled out the Jason Kidd/Nico Harrison special from a year ago, with Flagg running point next to four non-shooting threats in PJ Washington, Caleb Martin, Naji Marshall, and Daniel Gafford. Washington and Martin have solid percentages this season, but they don't warp the defense with their spacing, especially without a lead guard to disrupt the defense. Flagg was seeing two, three, and even four bodies in front of him inside the three point line on just about every drive.

This is a problem for the Mavericks. Flagg is obviously a special talent, but the apex version of him is usually unlocked when next to a competent lead guard that can get downhill. We saw this last season and we've seen it again this season, as Dallas has played very well with Kyrie Irving and Flagg on the floor together. This is no knock on Flagg, but playing him without a credible guard talent next to him is like trying to win a game with one arm tied behind your back.

Unfortunately Dallas hasn't prioritized a lead guard in the last two offseasons. Dallas used the ninth overall pick in this summer's draft for Morez Johnson Jr., another big and then brought in another big in Santi Aldama via trade. Dallas' only guard moves were a smaller move for end-of-the-bench prospect Marcus Sasser, who's shown flashes, and using a late first-rounder for Sergio de Larrea, who is currently injured. Walter Clayton Jr. joined the team a week before the season started on a two-way contract and was almost immediately the team's backup point guard. The Dallas guard rotation is bleak, and especially so when guys like de Larrea and Max Christie are injured.

Bottom line: the Mavericks have to prioritize the guard position over the course of the next 18 months, whether that's at the upcoming trade deadline, next summer's draft, or the ensuing offseason. It's going to hold this whole thing back until it's resolved.

It says a lot about how bad Dallas is as a defensive rebounding team when the Celtics can get 18 offensive boards on a night where they were shooting the leather off the ball. There weren't a lot of misses for the Celtics to go get, and yet they still found themselves with a sizable offensive rebounding advantage (18-11).

Dallas cannot be this big of a team and still fail to rebound the ball consistently. It's just an untenable combination. If the Mavericks want to be a big, lengthy, athletic team, then they have to play like it. It does the team no good to be this big as a roster and this bad at rebounding. The trickle down effects are apparent. Nothing else to say, except the Mavericks just have to be better.

#2877. Japan's economy unexpectedly contracts in third-quarter as elevated energy costs bite

Japan's economy unexpectedly contracted in the third quarter, shrinking at an annualized 0.1% compared to the previous quarter, as elevated energy prices weighed on consumption and net exports.

A Reuters poll of economists had estimated the economy to grow at 0.3% in the three months to September, while GDP had expanded at 1.4% in the previous quarter.

Japan's exports beat expectations for two of the three months in the quarter, but net exports weighed on the economy due to higher costs of energy imports, cutting 0.6 percentage point from the country's growth.

Energy subsidies for households had reportedly expired in September, exposing consumers to higher energy prices brought about by the Iran war.

The GDP data could complicate Japan's policy normalization path, though there's still a 75.6% chance of the Bank of Japan raising rates by 25 basis points in December, according to LSEG.

"Today's GDP data is hardly a green light for the BOJ to hike in December," according to Krishna Bhimavarapu, APAC economist at State Street Investment Management.

He pointed out that real final sales of domestic product fell 2.1% annualized, which is the largest decline outside the Covid-19 pandemic and the first quarter of 2022. "[This] looks to be a loss of momentum," he added. That metric is a measure of

Real final sales of domestic product refer to GDP growth, net of private inventories, and indicate underlying demand.

Marcel Thieliant, head of Asia Pacific at Capital Economics, still expects the BOJ to go ahead with the rate hike. "It looks increasingly likely that the Bank will have to tighten policy more aggressively than we've been anticipating as inflation is about to surge." The BOJ had declared that its 2% underlying inflation target had been met for the first time in 13 years at its meeting last month. Core inflation in September also hit its highest level this year at 2.1%, overshooting analysts' expectations.

On an year-on-year basis Japan's economy grew 1.1%, its strongest growth since the second quarter of 2025. Theliant noted that this was owed to a lower base in the third quarter of 2025.

Following the data release, the Nikkei 225 fell 0.62%, while the yield on the benchmark 10-year Japanese Government Bonds was at 2.885%. The yen weakened marginally against the dollar, trading at 159.76.

#2873. Gattuso: “Sogno lo stadio pieno. Sugli infortunati…”

Ai microfoni di Lazio Style Channel, mister Gennaro Gattuso ha commentato la vittoria in amichevole contro il Mgarr United

Ai microfoni di Lazio Style Channel, mister Gennaro Gattuso ha commentato la vittoria in amichevole contro il Mgarr United.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Cosa mi porto dietro dall'amichevole di oggi? Che non si è fatto male nessuno (ride, ndr). Scherzi a parte, siamo molto soddisfatti. I moduli contano poco, è la mentalità e la voglia di far fatica in campo. Rovella e Cataldi avevano bisogno, anche Doekhi e Gudmundsson.

Secondo me si può fare, Nicolò è dinamico, ha bisogno di condizione e mettere minutaggio. Si è allenato bene, si è dato da fare.

Lunedì faranno le risonanze Nuno, Zac e Frattesi, bisogna aspettare per vedere l'entità dei danni. Per questo stiamo temporeggiando, Zac sta già facendo qualcosa sul campo. Per Sutalo e Marusic aspettimo ancora un po' di giorni e usciremo con un comunicato quando saremo sicuri delle diagnosi.

I principi non cambiano, anche se ogni giocatore ha le sue caratteristiche. Servono intensità e voglia, così come oggi anche se era un'amichevole. Anche gli altri hanno infortunati, non dobbiamo piangerci addosso.

Deve fare uno switch, ci ho parlato ieri, ha età e potenziale. Deve migliorare sotto porta, stiamo lavorando, può fare molto di più.

Parlo e mi scontro molto con lui, può diventare uno dei migliori invasori del mondo. Io ogni volta che rubavo palla li contavo e per me erano tutti gol, sapevo che intorno avevo giocatori che con la palla erano più bravi di me. Lui è fondamentale, ma la differenza la stiamo facendo sull'attacco alla linea, ma non lo sta facendo solo lui. Arriva a fari spenti, non è un caso che sta facendo tutti questi gol. Ci scanniamo, ma ci vogliamo bene.

Il calcio è cambiato, le dinamiche che ho vissuto io da calciatore oggi non vanno più bene e devi sempre trovare la chiave giusta per entrare nella testa dei calciatori. Io non riesco a recitare, ho capito fin dal primo giorno dove venivo, sapevo che ci mettevo poco ad affezionarmi alle persone che sono qui, ai tifosi, al club. La storia della Lazio è incredibile, la gente è malata per la Lazio e poi ci rimani, il mio sogno è vedere la mia squadra con uno stadio pieno, festeggiare sotto la curva. Il calcio senza tifosi non è calcio, è qualcosa di diverso, i nostri tifosi sono coerenti e difficilmente torneranno, ma noi dobbiamo onorare la maglia. La speranza è l'ultima a morire. Gudmundsson? Non è brillantissimo, ma ha grandi giocate, ringraziamo che non ha avuto ricadute.

#2871. Why Lakers must figure out how to support Luka Doncic late in games

Luka Dončić continues to dominate as a scorer this season, but the Los Angeles Lakers will have to find a way to support their star player.

Lakers coach JJ Redick referred to Dončić as one of the league's best scorers before a game against the Phoenix Suns on Sunday, a sentiment shared by Giannis Antetokounmpo of the Miami Heat earlier this month.

Dončić nearly produced a double-double with 43 points on 13-of-28 shooting and nine assists for Los Angeles in the 131-123 road victory, living up to his coach's remarks.

Despite having one of the best records in the league (12-2), the third-year coach had other remarks and expectations for his team; he used the recent three-day break to share a 21-page analytics presentation with his players to serve as an internal audit on both offense and defense.

"A lot of good things and a lot of things to clean up," Lakers forward Ziaire Williams told reporters on Nov. 13. "Being first in the (Western Conference), with the stuff that they showed us, you would think we were almost last."

It is the Lakers' eighth straight win, with the last four coming on the road, but one of those aspects of the game that needs to be cleaned up is apparent.

Dončić and Austin Reaves, who was out Sunday due to injury, have proven they can win games together and can also take on a role as the high-scoring threat with the other out of the lineup.

Beyond the two stars, there's some inconsistency with the team's depth to help complement that scoring and allow its superstars an opportunity to rest.

Dončić ushered the Lakers to a quick start once again this season, scoring eight of the team's first 10 unanswered points. He finished the first quarter with 20 of the team's 33 points in the first quarter.

"We're working too hard and we're playing too hard to build these leads," Redick said during the postgame press conference on Sunday. "It's a disservice to each other to take your foot off the gas."

Los Angeles has held the lead in 76.8% of the game time this season, which is the best in the NBA, according to the Lakers. The team also has displayed its offensive dominance during its recent four-game stretch, having led for 92% of the game time.

The Lakers went wire-to-wire on Sunday, leading by as many as 31 points during the game, but allowed the Suns to battle back into the game with 43 points in the final frame.

"I wasn't planning to play in the fourth quarter," Dončić told reporters after the game. "We got to get better at that. I know we won eight in a row, but we got to get better at that.

"Once we get up, we got to stay there. We got to work on that."

Dončić gave credit to the Suns, who played aggressively while Los Angeles started the fourth quarter "a little bit sloppy."

Redick has stated multiple times this week that the Lakers' desire is to work toward being a top 10 defense in the NBA. The Lakers rank near the middle of the pack in terms of defensive rating, allowing close to 114 points per game.

After the game, Redick recalled a moment that could be featured in his next audit for the defense.

"We did a lot of good things through three quarters," Redick explained on Sunday. "... In the fourth, when (the Suns) are walking it up and we don't even talk and get matched up.

"They hit wide-open 3s and get going. You just can't allow that. You have to play for 48 minutes. We are going to need our defense to win us games."

The Lakers will have another chance to work toward Redick's defensive expectations against the Charlotte Hornets on Tuesday, Nov. 17 at 11 p.m. ET (8 p.m. PT).

#2869. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2868. How Nvidia turmoil and a Democratic Congress could reshape the AI trade, according to ETF experts

The largest technology stocks linked to the artificial intelligence trade could stand to benefit the most from Nvidia's woes and a Democratic-controlled Congress pushing back on data centers, according to investing experts interviewed on CNBC's "ETF Edge" earlier this week.

"We may have been put in an environment, as much as investors have sort of been looking for areas outside of Big Tech, where net-net Big Tech ends up as the winner once again," said Roundhill Investments CEO Dave Mazza, whose firm manages the Magnificent Seven ETF (MAGS).

Part of his bull case: Other players in the AI trade are positioned to fill Nvidia's chip demand if investors lose trust in the company after a tumultuous week that saw its iconic founder and CEO Jensen Huang resign.

"If we get into an environment where people can't trust … Nvidia, they're going to look for other sources for these chips because they need them," Mazza said during the "ETF Edge" interview, which occurred Monday as the Nvidia scandal grew but before Huang had resigned.

Discussing recent fund flow activity for the MAGS ETF, as well as for the Roundhill Neocloud ETF (NCLD) — which focuses on smaller market cap, specialized cloud computing companies — Mazza said, "In a weird way, [hyperscalers] may continue to benefit from this because they have the compute available. It's going to be more valuable if the demand is there."

The Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF ended the week in the green, albeit with a gain of less than 1%, while the Roundhill Neocloud ETF was up over 1%, though earnings from top holdings Coreweave and Nebius presented a mixed picture.

CoreWeave had its best day since April 2025 on Wednesday after its latest quarterly results, while Nebius shares sank after its Thursday report. The neocloud ETF remains deeply in the red since its August 2026 launch, down 39%, amid concerns about balance sheet strength, which could linger given Nvidia's role in financing the AI buildout. CoreWeave shares sank again on Friday, and Nvidia posted its longest losing streak since 2012 after its latest weekly losses.

Nvidia's interim CEO and current chief financial officer Colette Kress will have her first opportunity to reassure the market when Nvidia reports its third-quarter earnings next Tuesday. "Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders," Kress said in a statement issued on Thursday morning when the company announced Huang's exit.

Yorkville Ives partner and senior managing director Dan Ives said on "ETF Edge" on Monday that the market will remain optimistic about other AI chip players, like AMD and Alphabet, if Nvidia faces prolonged headwinds. Ives helped create the Dan IVES Wedbush AI Revolution ETF (IVES) while at Wedbush Securities, where he served as the managing director and global head of technology research before departing Wedbush to form his own firm earlier this year.

AMD finished trading this week up close to 13%, with Broadcom posting similar gains.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq index posted its best week since August as Nvidia's slump reshuffled the AI trade.

Yorkville Ives lowered its price target on Nvidia to $225 from $300 on Thursday after Huang's resignation, but maintained a buy rating on the stock, which finished the week at $162.85 per share.

"Nvidia remains the clear leader in GPU chips for the AI Revolution. They are roughly 2-3 years ahead of the nearest competitor and the demand trajectory the next few years is not changing because Jensen is gone," Ives wrote in a note to investors on Thursday. "The strategic roadmap is already essentially built around the next few family of chips and physical AI with 42k employees driving it. ... Valuation at roughly 10x 28 EPS is way too cheap once Nvidia gets through this storm and emerges on the other side."

The compute that does exist could become even more indispensable against the backdrop of a potential slowdown to the data-center buildout by Congress after the Democratic Party gains of Nov. 3 swept control of Congress, Mazza said.

Ives estimates that the "blue tidal wave" in Congress could mean 10% of planned data centers won't materialize on the low end, and 15%-20% on the high end, but he added that demand still outstrips supply four to one. "The compute is just that much more valuable in the capacity today than it was a week ago. So I could argue just more and more bullish, strong, get stronger," Ives said.

"The game is going to change for Big Tech. It will slow it down. But there's no overbuild, no bubble concerns. And I view that as actually a positive thing," he added.

#2867. Dylan Harper plays the hero as Spurs beat Jazz, extend win streak to 12

It was an ugly one and too close for comfort, but the Spurs got their 12th win in a row in their visit to Salt Lake City. Despite not playing particularly well, San Antonio escaped with a 110-102 victory to hand the Jazz their 12th straight loss, in large part thanks to Dylan Harper fourth-quarter heroics.

All the ingredients required for a strange game that could get more complicated than expected for the Spurs were there. It was a Sunday matinee; De'Aaron Fox was out; the Jazz had already played the Silver and Black twice in the young season; it was a one-game road trip at altitude. Yet it was hard to imagine just how weird things would be, especially in the first half. Mo Bamba turning into Stephen Curry was hard to predict, and so was Jordan McLaughlin being the first guy to reach double digits for the visitors. San Antonio's defense being lethargic early was no surprise but the way Utah was making them pay by hitting jumpers was. On the other end, meanwhile, Fox was missed in the halfcourt, so transition offense had to pick up the slack. It wasn't the most well-played bout of the season, but it was entertaining and close, as the score was tied after one.

The Spurs have been relying on their talent to overwhelm opponents even when the execution and occasionally the energy have been far from ideal, and to some degree, they achieved that against the Jazz in the first half. This time, however, getting a superstar performance from Wembanyama and a couple of other contributors to step up simply wasn't enough to create a lead, like it has in past games. Utah was confident after the strong start and didn't let a few missed jumpers discourage them. Defensively, they had no answers for The Alien, who had his three-point shot going, but were happy to sit back and watch others fall into bad habits and take the bait of driving or shooting when the other option would have been preferable. It was clear that San Antonio was going to have to take the win, because the Jazz were not going to give it away without a fight.

During two different stretches of the third, it seemed like the Spurs were finally going to impose their will. A strong start allowed them to carve a double-digit lead, but errant shooting and lackadaisical defense followed, and the home team quickly caught up. Then when the bench checked in, they managed to put together a rare run for a group that has struggled often, putting the Silver and Black up nine after an alley-oop dunk from the oft-maligned Luke Kornet. Alas, Utah once again rallied after a timeout, shooting their way back while San Antonio's jumpers refused to fall. Holding serve while the second unit was in would have normally been enough if the starters had come in rested to close the frame strong, but it just didn't happen. The game was tied going into a final period, and it didn't feel impossible to see two streaks being snapped on a shocker of an ending.

Fortunately, an overall strange game finished with the most logical result. The Jazz, who have struggled with maintaining leads, especially at the end of games, looked like the young, shorthanded team that they are in the clutch. Keldon Johnson, last year's Sixth Man of the Year, was the sparkplug San Antonio needed all game long, but elevated his impact with some crucial plays late. And Dylan Harper, who showed in the playoffs that he's not scared of the big moments, took over as the first option that Wembanyama couldn't be because of how much attention Utah was paying to his every move on offense. The young guard dropped 18 of his career-high 31 in the final period to keep the Spurs close enough to take advantage of the mistakes the home team would make down the stretch. It wasn't the prettiest of wins, but it was enough to keep the streak going.

What makes Dylan Harper such a scary young player is that a lot of what he does at this point in his career, on both ends, seems mostly done on instinct and sheer talent. He's a smart, polished player in some ways, but in others, he just… does things that happen to get good results more often than not. For most of the game he wasn't particularly efficient and at times forced things, but when the team actually needed someone to take over, that same hyper-aggressiveness and confidence that sometimes leads to poor decisions was the reason why he was ready to explode offensively. Harper is already a fantastic player who will only get better as a decision-maker, which should terrify every other team in the league.

While Harper's heroics will rightfully get most of the attention, Wembanyama's first half was also needed for the Spurs to escape with a win. Moreover, the Jazz' devoting so much attention to him on dives to the basket opened things up for others, including Harper on his consecutive fourth-quarter threes. The big guy did his job.

The third spot on the podium belongs to Keldon Johnson. In a game in which Devin Vassell and Julian Champagnie combined for 10 points on 17 shots and Harrison Barnes and Carter Bryant didn't move the needle off the bench, San Antonio needed someone who wasn't a guard or a center to step up. Johnson's made contributions in different areas, as accurately reflected by his stat line. A great mix of aggressiveness on offense and energy plays for a veteran who continues to reinvent himself.

The Spurs missed Fox, which isn't surprising, since he's their best half-court orchestrator, but Stephon Castle slotted in nicely in his old role as primary playmaker and had 20 points and nine assists. Jordan McLaughlin was decent off the bench as well, hitting some shots in the first half. Against better opponents, Fox's absence could be a bigger problem, but San Antonio survived without him.

The Spurs will kick off a three-game homestand by taking on one of the best teams in the East. It should be a good test for a San Antonio team that has not faced a lot of good opponents lately.

#2866. AI is reshaping banking jobs – but its ability to drive profits remains unclear

Banks' third-quarter results have brought their use of artificial intelligence into sharper focus, as lenders automate routine work, reshape hiring and test whether productivity gains can translate into revenue growth.

This earnings season, Barclays unveiled layoffs in certain functions where AI is being deployed; while UniCredit and Santander said technology was freeing employees for higher-value work and improving processes. It comes as U.S. giants such as JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs make similar changes in staffing and workflows.

But as banks pursue efficiency savings, strategists say evidence that AI is generating material, demonstrable revenue gains from it remains harder to pin down.

UniCredit CEO Andrea Orcel called AI an "opportunity to accelerate" that was allowing a shift toward more commercial, higher-value work and greater client engagement.

Orcel said the Italian lender — which reported its best-ever quarterly profit of 3.3 billion euros — is using the technology to "get us to the next level."

Orcel called AI "a lever that we didn't anticipate we would have, and working extremely well," on CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Nov. 9.

Santander said AI initiatives generated 194 million euros in business value in the first nine months of 2026. AI is now being embedded in "all the processes that we are conducting at the bank," said Chief Financial Officer José García Cantera.

"AI basically helps, at least for now, decrease low value-added processes, and the company employees can then focus on more value-added processes — this affects both revenue and costs," Cantera told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Oct. 28.

"It's difficult to just to say how much comes from AI, but clearly AI is improving the quality of our services to clients, is helping us in asset quality, in cost management, and is having clearly a very positive impact."

Dutch lender ABN AMRO is using AI to support anti-money-laundering checks and expanding its partnership with Infosys to modernize its technology, while preserving human judgement, CEO Marguerite Bérard said in the bank's third-quarter results on Nov. 11.

"These improvements make the bank easier to run and scale, and help colleagues spend more time on activities that create value for clients," she said.

Some lenders announced big headcount reductions during the third quarter, which are "partly, but not solely, due to AI," said Andrew Stimpson, managing director and head of European banks research at KBW.

"The cuts have tended to come from back-office and support functions rather than the front office," Stimpson told CNBC via email.

Barclays said it plans to cut 5,000 jobs — about 5% of its workforce — in 2026. Roughly 60% of the reductions will affect support functions, including developer roles, the British lender said in its third-quarter earnings update, though it sought to emphasize that AI is not responsible for all the cuts.

The Financial Times reported on Oct. 7 that HSBC could cut around half of management and specialist roles, and as many as 70% of financial adviser roles, in its U.K. wealth business as AI reshapes how it serves clients.

Goldman Sachs said it intends to "constrain headcount growth" and cut a limited number of jobs. JPMorgan managers have been told to avoid hiring as the bank deploys AI across its businesses, CFO Jeremy Barnum told analysts in the lender's third-quarter earnings call.

A KBW study published in September examined AI use across European banks. Customer service — including chatbots and sorting customer requests — was the most frequently cited use case, followed by behind-the-scenes functions such as operations, identity checks and preventing financial crime.

Cost savings remain the most commonly cited goal: just under half of the use cases KBW tracked were aimed at cutting costs or improving efficiency. The report found banks are increasingly exploring ways to grow revenue, while noting AI is not yet seen as a major driver of profits.

Wealth management and advisory are emerging as key tests of whether AI can boost bank revenues, rather than only lower costs, analysts say.

Banks seem "fairly aligned" that they can do this in wealth management, where AI could enable advisors "to serve a larger number of clients as AI makes onboarding and reporting much more efficient," said Stimpson.

Sophia Whitbread, an analyst at Ninety One, said the evidence is still strongest for AI improving service, engagement and employee productivity, rather than demonstrably generating revenue. But initiatives that let advisors use AI to support more clients could expand access to personalized guidance without a corresponding increase in relationship managers.

She said wealth management was likely "the most important revenue test because AI can augment rather than replace the advisor."

"For European banks, the probable model is 'human adviser plus AI', especially at UBS, Standard Chartered and the private-banking units of Deutsche, ABN and the French banks, rather than a fully automated relationship," Whitbread said, pointing to JPMorgan's advisor and wealth-planning tools as a U.S. example of that approach.

AI is also reducing "traditional project management and back-office opportunities," according to Mark Astbury, a director at recruitment consultant Morgan McKinley.

There is greater demand for "specialists in AI, software engineering and data," Astbury told CNBC via email.

"London's financial services sector is increasingly becoming a technology employer. Demand for talent hasn't disappeared, but the skills banks are investing in are changing."

As AI takes on more routine tasks junior employees traditionally handle, "banks will need to rethink how they develop the next generation of talent," he said.

"We're already seeing senior roles demanding specialist AI skills that go well beyond basic use of tools like Copilot or Gemini, and I expect entry-level positions will become increasingly technical too."

#2861. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.
