#2828. International student enrollment drops sharply, putting more U.S. colleges in financial jeopardy

Amid changing visa policies and rising degree costs, significantly fewer new international students enrolled at U.S. colleges this year — a shift that could have financial repercussions for colleges, students and the broader economy.

In the fall 2026 semester, the tally of new international students studying in the U.S. sank 15% compared to the prior academic year, according to a fall snapshot released Monday from the Institute of International Education, an international education nonprofit.

Over the 2025-26 academic year, the number of new international students in the U.S. fell more than 17%, largely because fewer graduate students came from countries such as India and China, the IIE's report found.

A growing number of U.S. colleges said that international student recruitment was a top concern, but the report also cited the high cost of a U.S. degree relative to other countries — along with uncertainty around new rules ending the traditional "duration of status" system and limiting most F-1 and J-1 visas to four years — as significant barriers.

Even though the Department of Homeland Security's new visa restrictions are currently on hold under a federal court injunction, experts say questions about the changing policies may have already discouraged some prospective international students.

"At a time when countries around the world are competing for talent, the United States should be strengthening our ability to attract the best and brightest, not creating new barriers to it," Fanta Aw, executive director and CEO of NAFSA: Association of International Educators, said in a Sept. 14 statement after a federal court temporarily blocked the Department of Homeland Security's new visa rules.

Foreign students typically pay full tuition, making international enrollment an important revenue source for colleges and universities, according to Robert Franek, editor-in-chief of The Princeton Review.

With fewer tuition dollars coming in, there are fewer resources for U.S. students, particularly for financial aid, he said: "There are going to be fewer research dollars, fewer scholarship dollars for students here in the states."

International students also contributed nearly $40 billion to the U.S. economy during the 2025-26 academic year, according to a separate NAFSA analysis. This year's enrollment decline is expected to cost the economy roughly $2.4 billion.

"The data again reflect the harsh reality that the barriers the U.S. government is putting in place to block talented international students are having a real impact," said NAFSA's Aw. "All Americans will feel the effects as international students' tuition payments, local spending, and contributions to U.S. excellence in research and innovation diminish."

Although the fallout extends well beyond college campuses, other research shows that colleges and the students they serve will bear the brunt of the impact.

Recent changes to F-1 student visa rules could heighten financial risks for some U.S. colleges and universities, particularly those that rely heavily on students from abroad, according to an analysis from Fitch Ratings released in August.

"Sustained drops in new international student enrollment can have outsized revenue effects, as international students often pay full tuition or receive less institutional aid than domestic students," Fitch analysts said in the report. "Lost revenue is hard to replace quickly."

Specifically, universities with large graduate and STEM — science, technology, engineering and math — programs, where time to degree completion can exceed four years, will have a harder time sustaining the enrollment pipeline and "may incur higher costs to address overseas recruitment challenges," the analysts wrote.

Most international students — 57% — study in STEM fields, according to IIE's report, so enrollment declines can have an outsized financial impact on schools with large engineering and computer science programs.

Underscoring the importance of those students, 91% of colleges and universities said recruiting international students in STEM fields is now a key priority, according to IIE's fall snapshot, which was based on a survey of 809 institutions.

#2827. Social Security and the blue wave: What could happen to payroll taxes and senior tax breaks

Congress has six years to enact changes to Social Security to prevent cuts to retirement benefits. Senators elected in the Nov. 3 midterms will be in office when the deadline arrives, and their decisions may be critical in making tax changes that can affect Social Security.

During the midterm campaign season, tax policy surfaced in the debate over Social Security in two different ways. Many Democrats, and some Republicans, called for high earners to pay more in Social Security payroll taxes, while many Republicans touted a recently enacted tax deduction for adults 65 and older.

The diverging tactics come at a crucial moment for reform: The first could increase revenue flowing into the program. The second reduces eligible older adults' income taxes and can lower the taxes some beneficiaries pay on Social Security benefits, reducing program revenue.

Social Security trustees' latest projections show the trust fund used to pay retirement benefits may start to become depleted in 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable.

Lawmakers do have some leeway — if the program's retirement and disability trust funds are combined, depletion would be pushed out to 2034, when 83% of scheduled benefits would be payable. But that's still a haircut for retirees — and it's coming soon.

"We've waited too long already, and the consequences are that the gap has grown larger," Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said of Social Security's shortfall.

On the campaign trail, midterm candidates largely avoided specifics on how they would reform Social Security. However, raising the payroll tax cap, also known as the taxable maximum, was a popular choice among Democratic candidates. That would require people who earn more than the cap, currently $184,500, to begin contributing payroll taxes for their earnings above that amount.

Meanwhile, some Republican candidates said they would extend the temporary senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per eligible individual that is set to expire in 2028.

The deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act passed in July of last year and billed as a senior bonus, was among the factors that moved Social Security trustees' retirement trust fund depletion projections three months sooner, according to an annual report released in June.

Here's what experts say could be in store now that Democrats have won control of the House and Senate.

Social Security is a pay-as-you-go system that relies on payroll taxes from workers to help make monthly benefit payments to millions of Americans.

In 2026, earnings up to $184,500 are subject to that tax. Workers and employers each contribute 6.2% of pay up to that threshold, while self-employed individuals pay 12.4%.

The wage cap will rise in 2027 to $192,300. It adjusts higher annually to reflect incremental changes in the average wage index.

On the campaign trail, some House and Senate candidates said the cap should be raised or eliminated entirely so high earners contribute more.

Following the election, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., who is expected to become the next chair of the Social Security subcommittee, said on X on Nov. 7 that his proposal to increase benefits by at least $2,400 per year and "scrap the cap" on earnings subject to Social Security taxes would be a "top priority."

Outgoing Sen. Bill Cassidy, R-La., responded on X that the plan would "put a 12.4% tax" on people who are not millionaires or billionaires.

The idea of higher payroll taxes has broader support. A national survey fielded in February found most respondents — 79% — support raising taxes for wages over $400,000, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. The change was the most popular option tested among the 2,509 adults surveyed.

Rising income inequality has meant the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings, though that percentage was expected to remain constant when lawmakers enacted the last major reforms to the program in 1983, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized at that level, according to the institute.

Despite candidates mentioning plans to change the cap during election season, some experts say Congress may put off tax hikes that could be politically unpopular.

"I think lawmakers may put some of these taxes on the table closer to 2032, when the Social Security trust fund hits insolvency," said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. "In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute."

If the payroll tax cap were lifted from just under $200,000 to $300,000 in earnings per year, that would cost someone at that higher income threshold about $6,700 more in direct payroll taxes, Riedl said.

"There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term," Riedl said. In the long term, though, "everything is on the table," she said.

While many Democrats have pushed for raising those taxes, some Republicans have also shared their openness to doing so.

"There's a crack in the dam there breaking," Dan Adcock, director of government relations and policy at the National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, said of the Republican support.

Even if lawmakers raise or eliminate the cap, they would still need to agree on other changes to shore up the program. Republicans may want benefit cuts such as raising the retirement age in exchange for increasing taxes, Adcock said.

Just how far adjusting the taxable maximum would go to help solve Social Security's funding woes depends on how it is applied.

If the cap is eliminated starting in 2027, with the full 12.4% tax applying to all earnings and no benefit credit for earnings above the current threshold, that would eliminate 58% of the long-range actuarial balance over 75 years, according to a September analysis by the Social Security Administration.

Applying the 12.4% payroll tax rate to earnings over $250,000 starting in 2027 could address 57% of the long-range balance, according to the SSA, provided there are no benefit increases in exchange for the extra contributions.

If the 12.4% payroll tax rate were applied to earnings over $400,000 starting in 2027 without benefit increases, that would eliminate 53% of the long-range actuarial balance, the agency's analysis found.

Eventually, all earnings would be subject to Social Security payroll taxes as the gap between the taxable maximum, which increases every year, and the new threshold closes over time.

Yet some experts say raising the payroll tax cap could have consequences for taxpayers and the economy.

Raising the cap would be an "unprecedented increase" in marginal tax rates, according to William McBride, chief economist at the Tax Foundation, an independent tax policy research organization.

Lifting the threshold could add more than 12 percentage points to the cost of earning an additional dollar of wages, McBride said. Those higher costs could affect the labor market, which may see higher unemployment as a result of the change, he said.

OBBBA's new senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per individual — or "bonus additional amount for seniors," as it was dubbed in an early version of the legislation — is in effect for tax years 2025 through 2028.

Lawmakers elected to Congress in the midterms may decide whether to extend the temporary tax break.

"2028 is going to be a presidential election year, and I would not expect either party to be willing to allow any senior tax cuts to expire while they're competing for senior votes," Riedl said.

When President Donald Trump signed OBBBA into law in 2025, the White House said in a release: "No tax on Social Security is a reality." However, the new law does not eliminate taxes on Social Security benefits.

Instead, it provides a deduction that reduces seniors' taxable income regardless of whether they have claimed Social Security benefits.

During the campaign, Republicans touted the $6,000 senior deduction as tax relief and criticized Democrats for voting against the law that enacted it. However, congressional Democrats unanimously opposed OBBBA, citing provisions including cuts to Medicaid and food assistance.

Some effects of the new $6,000 senior deduction are different from those of eliminating taxes on benefits, according to McBride at the Tax Foundation. The senior deduction affects lower- and middle-income taxpayers to a greater degree, while eliminating taxes on Social Security benefits would be more beneficial to upper-income taxpayers, McBride said.

How Social Security benefits are currently taxed

Individuals with $25,000 to $34,000 in combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest and half of annual Social Security benefits — pay federal taxes on up to 50% of their Social Security benefits. For individuals with over $34,000 in combined income, up to 85% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax.

For married couples filing jointly with between $32,000 and $44,000 in combined income, up to 50% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax. For married couples with more than $44,000 in combined income, that increases to up to 85% of their benefits.

The senior bonus tax deduction allows those age 65 and older to deduct up to $6,000 for individuals, or up to $12,000 for married couples. The deduction phases out for modified adjusted gross income over $75,000 for single filers and $150,000 for married couples.

The senior bonus deduction — together with the tax year 2025 standard deduction of $15,750 for single taxpayers and $31,500 for married couples and the existing senior deduction for those who qualify of $2,000 for singles and $3,200 for married couples — "amounts to the largest tax break in history for America's seniors," the White House said in July 2025. In 2026, the standard deduction is $16,100 for single filers and $32,200 for married couples.

Under OBBBA, 88% of beneficiaries, or 51.4 million individuals age 65 and older receiving Social Security income, will have exemptions and deductions that exceed their taxable Social Security income, according to the administration. Prior to the law, 64% of beneficiaries, or 37.2 million individuals, had tax breaks that exceeded their taxable Social Security income.

But the measure does nothing to resolve Social Security's depletion problem.

In an annual report released in June, Social Security's trustees cited OBBBA as having a "substantial effect" on the program's financial status because beneficiaries will pay less in income taxes on benefits. In that report, the trustees moved the projected depletion date for the program's retirement trust fund to the fourth quarter of 2032 — up one quarter from the previous year's projections.

"This new deduction for seniors cuts into some of the funding for the trust fund, so it makes the solvency date a little nearer in time," McBride said. "It makes the program a little more fragile, if you will."

#2826. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2825. ‘Standing on one leg’: IMF chief warns about global economy as AI circular financing hits $1 trillion

The global economy is "on the AI leg," International Monetary Fund Managing Director Kristalina Georgieva said Monday, warning that the overdependence on the sector risks the world's financial stability.

Georgieva flagged that a "sudden drop in investment confidence and withdrawal from the AI field" could weaken economic growth, speaking with CNBC's JP Ong on the sidelines of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations' summit in the Philippines.

"We have a world economy that is standing on one leg on the AI leg. If this leg wobbles, that can lead to financial stability troubles," Georgieva warned.

Circular financing among AI companies — where money invested in an AI company ultimately flows back to the investor through purchase orders — has grown to a little over $1 trillion, two-thirds of it funded by debt, the IMF estimates. "It's not a huge size, but it is a rapid increase," Georgieva said.

Nvidia Corp, which has seen more than $1 trillion wiped out of its market value over the past month, is at the center of these circular financial deals. The fallout from the chipmaker's former CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy, has led to a sell-off in global AI-linked stocks and put the spotlight on the company's role as a key financier of the AI infrastructure buildout.

UBS analyst Tim Arcuri estimated in a recent note that an Nvidia financing pullback could easily remove $50 billion or more out of the system, at a time when rising interest rates have already made corporate borrowing more expensive.

Georgieva's warning comes as the AI build-out has been a critical engine of growth for the global economy, propping up trade and investment.

The IMF raised its 2026 global growth forecast to 3.1% in October as major economies lean on AI investment to power growth otherwise curtailed by the energy crunch caused by the Iran war.

The institution estimated that about 40% of the world's workforce will be affected by AI. Capital Economics suggested that as much as 80% of the recent expansion in global exports was concentrated in AI-related goods.

Investors' frenzy for AI reflects expectations running ahead of what the technology has proven to deliver, Georgieva said. "We tend to rush ahead," as valuations climb, she said, therefore "some corrections" become necessary.

IMF in October had warned that a short-term disappointment could set off a disorderly correction across asset classes: in its worst case scenario, if AI fails to live up to the market's lofty expectations, U.S. equity prices could fall 20% in 2026 and global output end up 1.1% below its baseline estimate by 2028.

Global equites, including U.S. chipmakers, have been seeing a bruising sell-off over the past few weeks, with investor appetite for the AI trade facing a fresh test this week ahead of earnings from Nvidia and Anthropic's blockbuster listing.

Georgieva on Monday characterized the recent market swings as "orderly," and unlikely to spiral into anything that could undermine the world economy, while urging caution. "We have to be watchful."

#2824. Bitcoin slides to $62,000 following liquidation cascade, then rebounds slightly

Bitcoin fell to its lowest levels since August over the weekend as a wave of forced liquidations accelerated amid broader weakening in market liquidity and risk appetite.

The price of the flagship cryptocurrency fell as low as $62,000 late Saturday, according to Coin Metrics, and is coming off its sixth weekly decline in a row. On Monday morning, it had climbed back to the $64,000 level.

Bitcoin's ongoing weakness pushed its price below key technical support levels during the week, breaking below its 200-day moving average of about $73,000 and triggering the cascade of long liquidations, forcing traders to sell their assets at market price to settle their debts, pushing prices lower.

On Saturday night, exchanges tracked by CoinGlass saw more than $165 million in liquidated bitcoin long positions, following $629 million on Friday and nearly $700 million on Thursday.

Bitcoin was also facing potential selling pressure ahead of Anthropic's anticipated initial public offering, with the listing drawing speculative capital away from riskier assets.

On Friday, the dollar index – which has an inverse relationship with bitcoin – reached its highest level since April 2025, following hotter-than-expected inflation earlier this week that increased expectations of further Fed tightening. That pushed Treasury yields and the dollar higher while pressuring both bitcoin and gold, despite their appeal as alternative stores of value in the longer term.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from the combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen.

"A break below $60,000 is possible if Nvidia's results add to the tech selling," he said. "The catalysts for a move higher are mostly macro, and a softer dollar with falling real yields would ease the pressure on every risk asset at once. If that happens, bitcoin goes into the recovery with less leverage than it carried in October, and a close back above $73,000 would be the first sign that the selling is over."

Edwin Mata, CEO of tokenization platform Brickken, said he's monitoring whether spot buyers step in once the forced selling subsides.

"Clearing out leveraged longs can slow the selling," he said. "For a recovery to hold, spot buyers need to keep showing up after the forced selling has passed."

#2822. China confirms Xi-Trump-Putin historic summit. What will be on the agenda?

China confirmed Monday that President Xi Jinping will meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin and U.S. President Donald Trump in Shenzhen on Thursday, setting up the first-ever gathering of the three leaders.

That confirmation from a spokesperson at China's Foreign Ministry follows announcements from Moscow and the White House. The Wall Street Journal had reported last month that Putin had proposed a three-way sit-down in an attempt to negotiate an end to the four-year war in Ukraine.

While the summit among the three nations, on the sidelines of the APEC meeting in Shenzhen, is unprecedented, the leaders have held bilateral meetings over the years. Xi has met with Trump three times since October last year and more than 40 times with Putin since becoming president in 2013. Trump and Putin have met in person on several occasions, including a visit to Alaska by the Russian leader in August 2025.

"For Xi, just the ability to bring Putin and Trump to the table, a first among equals scenario, will deliver much of the political benefit even before the discussions begin," said Peter Alexander, Shanghai-based managing director at Z-Ben Advisors.

Trump has departed Washington, D.C. for a four-day trip to Asia that also includes a stop in the Philippines.

The main hurdle to the first-of-its-kind summit now appears to be "a disagreement over the agenda," said Neo Wang, chief China strategist at Evercore ISI.

The White House signaled the U.S. intention to prioritize talks over nuclear non-proliferation and the Middle East war, while downplaying the prospects of engaging in talks over Ukraine — a topic high on Moscow's agenda.

"He [Trump] will discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world, whose proliferation President Trump believes is very dangerous," Olivia Wales, a White House spokeswoman, told reporters on a briefing call. The president will raise the U.S.-Israeli war against Iran in the meeting but is not expected to bring up Russia's war in Ukraine, the official said.

China has no interest in engaging in nuclear disarmament talks until it achieves parity with the U.S. in about 10 years, said David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy.

Putin's position on the war appears unwavering, with the Russian president telling U.S. envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff in a meeting in Moscow last week that Europe should "figure out" whether "they want peace or war." The meeting came a day after an earlier round of talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine wrapped up in Abu Dhabi.

Russian presidential aide Yuri Ushakov said last week the talks in Shenzhen would continue the diplomatic efforts aimed at reaching a negotiated settlement to the Ukraine war, according to state-controlled local media Russia Today.

The summit will likely yield little concrete result, but presents an "almost 100% photo op" that lifts China's status and power at the top table, said Roche, while Trump's political standing slides. Nvidia and the chip-smuggling issues involving a suspected Chinese spy will stay off the agenda, as Trump steers clear of thorny issues where he is unlikely to claim success.

China and Russia could turn more aggressive with their military ambitions over the next two years if they perceive Trump as politically weakened, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC earlier this month, following the dramatic defeat of Trump's Republican Party in the midterm election.

China did not specify what will be discussed at the three leaders' meeting in Monday's statement.

"Xi may be inclined to do Putin a favor by showing up, given close strategic ties," said Wang. But a joint statement on Ukraine territory, security arrangements or sanctions against Russia appears unlikely, he said, as it would leave Beijing "more exposed to political scrutiny and accountability."

A U.S.-China trade truce set to expire in January is likely to be on the table, with analysts largely expecting an extension despite signs of growing friction in the bilateral relationship.

Aside from an immediate extension, leaders may also look to assess "whether a more durable framework for a rebalancing of U.S.–China trade relations can be established," said Carlos Casanova, senior economist for Asia at UBP.

U.S. trade deficit with China remains substantial, with Beijing's exports to Washington recording double-digit growth for most of this year, from last year's low base. Narrowing that deficit will be central to Washington's call for a more balanced trade, Casanova said.

#2821. A major NFT marketplace is shutting down—5 years after the boom, there's a lesson for investors

Five years ago, NFTs seemed to be everywhere.

Although the digital collectibles, known as nonfungible tokens, had existed since 2014, they exploded in popularity in 2021 alongside the cryptocurrency boom, becoming status symbols for celebrities and wealthy collectors.

Weekly NFT trading volume across major blockchains surged from about $4.4 million in late 2020 to a peak of $3.2 billion in August 2021, according to CryptoSlam data published by The Block.

Today, much of that trading activity has disappeared. Weekly NFT trading volume has fallen more than 99% since August 2021, and several major NFT marketplaces have closed. On Thursday, Blur, once the largest marketplace for Ethereum-based NFTs, shut down its website.

At the height of the boom, Twitter co-founder Jack Dorsey sold an NFT of his first tweet for $2.9 million, while a digital artwork by an artist known as Beeple fetched $69.3 million at Christie's. Paris Hilton and Jimmy Fallon showed off their Bored Ape NFTs on "The Tonight Show," and even McDonald's released NFTs celebrating the return of its McRib sandwich.

Back then, some buyers believed NFTs represented the future of digital ownership, while others bet they could resell them at higher prices. But as enthusiasm faded, so did the demand that had driven the digital tokens' values higher.

The NFT boom and bust shows how quickly speculative investments can unravel when demand dries up. For investors, it's a cautionary tale about spotting the warning signs before buying into the next craze, financial experts tell CNBC Make It.

Part of the initial appeal of NFTs was the promise of a new way for artists and other creators to make money from their work. Using blockchain technology, NFTs allowed creators to sell digital collectibles with verifiable ownership records and potentially earn royalties on subsequent sales through smart contracts.

More broadly, NFTs were touted as part of the future of digital ownership, with the potential to transform how people bought, sold and traded digital assets.

But establishing ownership of a digital collectible was different from creating lasting demand for it, says Evan Mills, a financial advisor at wealth management firm Scholar Advising.

"NFTs tried to manufacture that demand," he says. "Blockchain could establish who owns the token, but it couldn't guarantee that anyone would really value the ownership at the end of the day."

In practice, many of the NFTs that attracted attention were relatively simple digital images, such as cartoon animals or promotional artwork featuring beer cans.

Traditional collectibles, such as paintings, antiques and baseball cards, have established histories and communities of collectors that can sustain demand, Mills says. NFTs, by comparison, became enormously popular in a relatively short period.

Unlike many traditional collectibles, however, NFTs could be created in virtually unlimited quantities. While individual collections might contain a limited number of tokens, there was little stopping creators from launching new series. At the height of the boom in 2022, roughly 500 new NFT collections were being created every day, according to blockchain analytics firm IntoTheBlock.

The rapid rise in NFT prices helped fuel expectations that buyers would continue paying more for them. But as enthusiasm faded, so did the pool of potential buyers.

And unlike stocks or bonds, most NFTs didn't generate income, meaning buyers hoping to turn a profit generally depended on someone else being willing to pay more for them, says Martin Mulyadi, the YHB Endowed Chair in Accounting at Shenandoah University.

"Most NFTs paid nothing, so the only way to make money was finding a next buyer at a higher price," he says. "When the buyers stopped coming, there was nothing holding up the price."

For everyday investors, the NFT bust is a reminder that an asset's popularity doesn't necessarily make it a worthwhile investment.

One common justification for buying risky investments like collectibles is that they make up only a small portion of an otherwise diversified portfolio. Financial advisors often recommend limiting speculative holdings to money an investor can afford to lose. But keeping an investment small doesn't necessarily make it worthwhile, Mulyadi says.

"While keeping a bet small limits the damage, it doesn't turn a weak asset into a good one," he says.

Mills argues that investors can also confuse speculation with diversification.

"Diversification is not just owning more things," he says. "It's owning things that have different economic characteristics that change the risk and return of your overall portfolio."

For collectibles, that means looking beyond an item's scarcity or popularity to whether there's an established market of buyers likely to value it over time. Even then, future demand can be difficult to predict.

There's also a difference between buying a collectible because you actually like it and buying it because you think someone else will pay more for it. For many NFT speculators, the appeal was likely less about owning what was essentially a JPEG than the prospect of reselling it for a profit.

Before buying a speculative asset, Mills recommends asking two questions: What is the economic reason it should generate a return, and what role does it play in the overall portfolio? If an investor can't answer those questions, he says, the investment may not belong in the portfolio at all.

"A bad investment doesn't become a good investment just because you only own a little bit of it," Mills says.

#2820. 10 things we learned from Patriots vs. Lions

For a second week in a row, the New England Patriots got blown out by an NFC North team on Sunday. Once again, they looked listless and disengaged for the majority of the game.

Let's get into our lessons learned from the Patriots' 35-10 loss to the Detroit Lions in Germany.

The Patriots haven't had many playmakers on offense this year, but Drake Maye has still put together a (mostly) solid year. Without him, however, it looks like one of the worst offenses in football.

Sunday's game was concrete proof of that. Tommy DeVito couldn't get the ball to open receivers, separation was on and off, and they couldn't run the ball. If Maye misses time after the bye, and it's looking increasingly likely that that is going to be the case, it's going to be tough to watch.

Speaking of Tommy DeVito, after Drake Maye went out with an injury, the Patriots were forced to turn to him. The media likes DeVito and the Patriots clearly like him too, since they gave him a contract extension this offseason. Unfortunately, he just isn't that good of a quarterback.

On Sunday, he missed open receivers left and right and on more than one occasion threw guys directly into contact. I understand that he's the backup inserted on short notice, and that the Patriots aren't asking him to be a great player because they really don't want him playing at all, but it would be nice if he was at least able to competently run the offense. Against Detroit, he wasn't.

A.J. Brown's inability to do anything positive this year has been almost laughable. He got hurt in the first game, missed a good chunk of time on injured reserve, came back, was a non-factor, and then got hurt again. He returned on Sunday, was unable to get open and had a drop as well. He finally made a nice catch-and-run to move the stick on a third down, and then promptly went to the locker room with a hamstring injury.

It's been a rough year for Brown, and the Patriots front office: they effectively bid against themselves to trade for him and still gave up a first-round pick in 2028 to do so. Unless he somehow manages a complete 180, the trade is going to go down as a massive failure, and potentially hamstring the Patriots for years to come.

Their defense was the reason the Patriots made the Super Bowl last year, and the hope was that it would take another step forward in 2026. Unfortunately, that has not happen and the unit has instead taken a step back.

It did register a couple of turnovers on Sunday, but the unit was unable to stop its opponent more or less all day long. They came out without any fire, missed tackles all over field, and put the Patriots in a hole right away.

We all were impressed with the job that Zak Kuhr had done last year, but the league has seemed to figure him out, and whatever he's doing isn't working. Yes, they have dealt with some injuries, but, for the most part, the defense has been fairly healthy. They have been out-coached, out-played, and, to be frank, it oftentimes looks like the other team simply wants it more than they do.

With this defense, the Patriots aren't going anywhere, especially if Drake Maye ends up missing time.

Christian Gonzalez is one of the best corners in the NFL, and surely one of the best players on the Patriots. The last two weeks, however, saw him have back-to-back rough games for the first time in his career.

On the game's first third down against the Lions, his struggles continued, when Amon-Ra St. Brown caught a pass in the flat, shook Gonzalez into oblivion, and then took the ball 56 yards for a touchdown. It was a bad start for Gonzalez, who later also fell victim to a pick play on another St. Brown score.

it wasn't all bad, however. He also registered an interception on a play where he ran the route for St. Brown and undercut him for the pick. It was a play that showed the player he has been, and will try to be again.

While we are on the topic of interceptions, the Patriots have finally won the turnover battle for the first time this season. They picked off Jared Goff twice, and only turned it over once.

In a game where they got embarrassed again, and with everything looking absolutely terrible in New England now, I'll take any positive notes I can get. Now, if they only were able to go a game without turning the ball over…

The NFL is filled with players who are really fast, really strong, and really elusive. None of those players, however, measure up to Gibbs. His ability to stop and start is ridiculous, and his speed is nothing short of blazing.

He leads the NFL in touches, and there is no sign of him slowing down. People had thought that TreVeyon Henderson would be the Patriots' version of Gibbs, but there is no one in this league like him, and, just like most teams that have played the Lions this year, New England had no chance of stopping him on Sunday.

In fact, he ended up out-gaining them by himself: Gibbs had 153 rushing yards and 99 receiving yards for a total of 252 yards. The Patriots offense finished the day with a grand total of 151.

The Patriots activated Gilliam off of injured reserve before the game, and they were hoping he would be a factor in the running game and on special teams. Instead, the Patriots couldn't run the ball to save their lives, Gilliam twice ran into his return man when he was trying to block, and he also committed two penalties on special teams.

Personally, I didn't love activating him off the IR, and, for Sunday at least, the Patriots didn't get any better with him on the field. With Drake Maye set to miss time, however, they might have to start relying on him and the running game.

Mike Vrabel did a fantastic job last year, there is no denying it. The culture he was trying to build was obvious, and the result was a mentally and physically tough team that reveled in the opportunity to go on the road and prove itself.

This year, however, the team has been a disaster. There have definitely been a lot of injuries, but the mental toughness has almost completely disappeared. The amount of pre-snap penalties is alarming, and the unforced errors in judgement and physical play have been bad. To go along with that, the way that he and some of the players have been speaking to the media is confusing as well.

If you're going to be a culture guy, then the culture has to matter, and it doesn't look good right now. I'm not saying that they should fire Vrabel or anything, but it bears watching. If the team doesn't start playing better, and they don't fix the fundamentals (turnovers, penalties, tackling), then there might be a reason to move on sooner rather than later.

Obviously, not every team is going to be great every year, but he has hand-picked a lot of these guys for the culture here because he coached them before. If this team has any sort of culture issue, it's squarely on Vrabel's shoulders.

(I also think that it's notable that they seemingly rushed A.J. Brown back twice instead of giving him a long rest, and he's been hurt both times. Maybe that's the medical staff, but it's ultimately the coaching staff who makes the decision to play the guys, even if they are physically cleared.)

Perhaps no team in the NFL has needed a bye week as much as the Patriots do. They played two bad games in a row, have a ton of injuries, including at quarterback, and desperately need to get their minds right and get healthy. The Patriots are going to have to come up with something significantly better than what they've brought lately, because they are getting crushed and look like a team on the verge of giving up.

A reset over the bye seems necessary, and might help them get back to a spot where they look like a competent and competitive football team.

#2819. Anthropic’s IPO lands as the embattled AI trade looks for a much-needed bright spot

Between rising interest rates, a nationwide AI backlash, a major political shift in Washington, D.C., and the sudden ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the tech industry is under mounting strain.

Now comes the most important tech IPO in years, and a defining event for the burgeoning but still young artificial intelligence market.

With Anthropic expected to debut on the Nasdaq on Thursday, the stakes are sky-high for almost every major tech company. Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company are collectively investing $26 billion at the IPO price, which is currently expected at $70 to $78 a share. Some of those companies are existing investors, as is early backer Amazon, whose stake could be in the hundreds of billions of dollars.

In addition to all the cash on the line, tech giants also need Anthropic's spending for many years to come. The Claude creator and its top rival, OpenAI, are driving an outsized amount of the AI buildout, and are burning historic amounts of money in the process.

Anthropic has outstanding spending commitments of over $900 billion, according to its latest prospectus. It's on the hook to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon over the course of a decade, and another $400 billion with other companies for cloud capacity, according to filings and partner disclosures. The lab also made $300 billion in commitments with data center and chip partners. Anthropic intends to lay out $125 billion to $150 billion for AI chips from Google, though delivery won't start until 2028.

"The numbers are just staggering," Joe Fath, a partner at venture firm Eclipse and former T. Rowe Price portfolio manager, said in an interview. "It's hard to get your head wrapped around it."

Cloud partners Amazon, Google and Microsoft account for 31% of contractual obligations, Anthropic said in its prospectus. Last year, Amazon built an Indiana data center just for Anthropic, using custom chips.

By going public now, Anthropic can raise a large portion of the money it needs to pay for the technology and infrastructure required for building and hosting its frontier models and expanding portfolio of services. The company recorded a net loss of $104 billion through the first nine months of 2026. The IPO could yield over $90 billion in proceeds, on top of Anthropic's $132 billion in existing cash, including short-term investments.

The length of Anthropic's contracts is also unique. Typically such contracts last five years or less, while Anthropic's agreement with Amazon extends for twice that amount of time.

"Realistically, anything past three to four years, you're basically throwing darts in the dark," said Corey Quinn, chief cloud economist at spend management software vendor Duckbill. "It is almost a certainty that it will be renegotiated long before the culmination of that contract."

Meanwhile, a data center for Anthropic that starts leasing in 2027 has a 20-year term. For both space and computing, Anthropic is splurging now so it doesn't end up short on capacity long into the future.

"In the past, we have significantly underestimated our compute requirements, which left us capacity constrained and impacted both product development and deployment," Anthropic said in the prospectus.

Anthropic and Amazon didn't provide a comment.

It's a tough time for Anthropic to hit the Nasdaq, with the offering coming as tech investors are desperate for some good news. The Nasdaq is down to start November after dropping 5.3% in October, its worst month since March 2025.

Rising interest rates pushed investors out of debt-heavy neoclouds, which have become a critical piece of the infrastructure buildout. And in the midterm elections earlier this month, Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress and in key gubernatorial races, while dozens of ballot measures were passed that will potentially limit new data center development across the country.

Then came the Nvidia crisis. Shares of the chipmaker at the center of the AI boom tumbled 13% this week to their lowest since July 2025, after Huang's multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, led to his ouster early Thursday. Huang was replaced on an interim basis with CFO Colette Cress, who faces a major test on Tuesday, two days before the Anthropic debut, as Nvidia is set to report quarterly results.

The ongoing Nvidia fallout and the Anthropic IPO create quite the split-screen moment for tech's heavyweights, which are closely tied to both companies.

Google will be paying particularly close attention.

The company is set to own about 13% of Anthropic after the IPO, and is backstopping the lab's $19 billion in lease commitments to Terawulf, which will supply data center space in Kentucky. If Anthropic goes insolvent, Google will step in and pay the remaining rent or a termination fee, Terawulf said.

"Everybody knows that Anthropic is going to be the future of Google," said Subhadeep Chatterjee, a former Google cloud partnerships leader who now advises companies.

One major impetus for Anthropic to enter costly and long-term guarantees is that OpenAI is being equally aggressive, and capacity is limited, particularly for graphics processing units. Going long can mean locking in rates as the industry confronts questions about whether data center development will slow.

"You will need to be in a rush to get infrastructure, and if you are not going to be there, you will be out of business," said Eli Mansoor, CEO of OskaQ Consulting, which advises companies on cloud negotiations. "It used to be that prices always go down, but this is changing, especially in the GPU world."

Big financial firms are lining up to help Anthropic get what it wants. Bank of America, Blackstone, Citigroup and Morgan Stanley have offered $63 billion to buy racks full of Broadcom AI chips that Anthropic will rent. Broadcom has provided a backstop with a maximum liability of $43 billion.

That's a lot of silicon and a massive bet for a five-year-old company that's never lived through a technology downturn.

"It's not a risk-free prospect, but I would call it smart investing," said David Tollen, founder of Sycamore Legal, where he specializes in IT contracts. "They're investing, in a sense, in themselves. They're putting money into something that if it succeeds, they get a piece of the action."

WATCH: Buying Anthropic means betting its explosive growth can outpace its expenses, says Jim Cramer

#2818. How much longer can Jordan Ott ignore the obvious?

The Los Angeles Lakers have been one of the best teams in the NBA through the early stages of the season, entering Phoenix riding a seven-game winning streak and sporting an 11-2 record. As the game progressed and Los Angeles built a 31-point lead, I resigned myself to the fact that the chasm between their excellence and the Suns' mediocrity was painfully apparent. Phoenix still had a long way to go holistically before becoming a consistently competitive basketball team.

The Suns' second unit reminded us of what Phoenix Suns basketball can look like. They ripped off a 41-16 run, pulling within five points in the closing minutes. Luka Doncic, a player who seemingly possesses the ability to spit in your face and laugh at you while doing it, had to take the ice packs off and check back into the game to ensure a victory for Los Angeles. Considering where things stood earlier in the afternoon, that's a small victory in and of itself.

What Sunday afternoon ultimately reinforced, however, was how fundamentally broken the Phoenix Suns' starting lineup is. Because the brand of basketball we've come to associate with this team is alive and well in Phoenix. We saw it in the fourth quarter. The energy, the defensive aggression, the connectivity, and the collective determination to make life uncomfortable for the opposition. Unfortunately, those characteristics are often masked by questionable roster construction, duplicative player archetypes in the starting five, and a lack of clearly defined roles.

There's an identity within this basketball team waiting to emerge with greater consistency, and we continue to see flashes of it when Jordan Ott turns to his bench. Meanwhile, the starting unit remains heavily dependent on Devin Booker's ability to manufacture productive stretches of basketball through his individual brilliance. When he can't, the unit begins to crumble under the weight of its own deficiencies, particularly its inability to rebound the basketball.

How much longer can you continue rolling out a starting five that routinely puts your second unit in a position where it has to resuscitate the basketball team?

#2817. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2816. Cantwell urges Lutnick to stop AI chip sales to China ahead of Xi meeting

Sen. Maria Cantwell, the top Democrat on the Senate commerce committee, alleged in a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Sunday that the department under his leadership failed to prevent China from accessing the U.S.'s most advanced artificial intelligence chips.

In the letter, Cantwell prodded Lutnick to enforce export controls and stop the flow of AI chips to companies barred from receiving the technology. She sent the missive ahead of a planned meeting this week between U.S. officials and Chinese President Xi Jinping on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation, in China.

Her urging comes as Washington reels from revelations that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had a relationship with an alleged Chinese spy named Alice Huang. Nvidia is the leading U.S. company designing advanced AI chips.

Alice Huang was an executive at Singaporean technology company Megaspeed that allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China.

Lutnick, meanwhile, has come under fire for stalling efforts to crack down on chip sales to China and for his close personal relationship with Jensen Huang, who was a vocal opponent of export controls.

"The Department of Commerce has resisted efforts to restrict companies flagged as national security threats, weakened safeguards, and left open pathways for the PRC to train advanced AI models using sensitive American technology," Cantwell wrote to Lutnick. "These failures to carry out the law help give away America's technology advantage in AI."

The Commerce Department did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Monday.

Cantwell called on Lutnick to use his authority to enforce a 2018 law, known as the Export Control Reform Act, which gives the Commerce Department broad latitude to enforce export controls and protect sensitive American technology.

She also cites potential financial conflicts involving Lutnick's sons, who control Cantor Fitzgerald, which she said held $1.2 billion in Nvidia stock at the time of his nomination. And she notes the Trump administration's 25% fee on certain Nvidia chips sold to China, which she said could create "a perverse financial incentive to approve exports of sensitive American technology."

"America's national security and AI leadership cannot be compromised by family business interests or a policy that generates federal revenue from exporting technology critical to our competitive edge," Cantwell wrote.

Both Democratic and Republican members of Congress have ramped up pressure on Lutnick and other Commerce Department officials after a Nov. 9 New York Times report alleging the department may have slow-walked an investigation into Megaspeed.

On Thursday, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December.

Lutnick has agreed to appear before the panel, Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement last week.

And Democrats, who will take control of both the House and Senate in January, have signaled they will investigate Jensen Huang and Commerce officials.

On Wednesday, Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the top Democrat on the Senate banking panel, and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

The letter gives all three officials until Nov. 17 to confirm that preservation directives have been issued.

#2815. 5 things we learned from the Chiefs feasting on Dirty Bird

For most of the first half, it looked like the Kansas City Chiefs were the better team, even if the offense on the field didn't reflect that. Then, just before halftime, the Chiefs trotted Harrison Butker out to attempt a field goal — and he made it!

Harrison Butker actually made a field goal.

An Atlanta Falcons player jumped offside.

The extra five yards gave the Chiefs a first down. The very next play, quarterback Patrick Mahomes threw a dime to his brand-new target, Josh Downs, who brought in the pass for a touchdown.

Here are five things we learned from the Chiefs' 35-13 victory over the Falcons.

I say this a little tongue-in-cheek. We've known this team was better than their 2025 iteration pretty much from the jump. But it's like they say, you are only as good as your record. And up until now, the Chiefs had not surpassed their disappointing 6-11 record from a year ago.

With this week's drubbing of the hapless Falcons, the Chiefs have amassed their seventh win of the season, ensuring that no matter what happens the rest of the way out, they've locked up a better record than last year.

Now, the only question that remains is: how much better are they?

A lot has been said about the decline in the Chiefs' air yards per attempt in recent years, including their modest 4.15 air yards per attempt over the last four games heading into Atlanta.

Over the years, we've heard every speculated reason for why the Chiefs couldn't seem to find success throwing the deep ball.

Some said it was because we traded away Tyreek Hill.

Some said it was because Mahomes had lost his ability to throw the ball 20+ yards accurately.

Some blamed it on coaching and playcalling.

Some said it was a deficit of talent at the receiving position.

Some said teams adapted to take away the deep ball.

Most said it was a combination of all four — but we now have definitive empirical evidence that rules Mahomes out of the equation. On Sunday, Mahomes completed six out of seven passes downfield.

Mahomes never lost the ability to throw the ball deep — he just needed the pieces to fall back into place for it to make sense to air it out.

We knew the Chiefs needed help at wide receiver.

We knew that the Chiefs had their eyes set on trading for wide receiver Josh Downs from the Indianapolis Colts.

And when they pulled off the trade just before the deadline, we all crossed our fingers and prayed he was the missing piece to this passing attack. And while the jury is still out on whether the issues around this passing attack are fully solved, I think it's fair to say Downs looks like everything we were hoping for.

Sure, it's a small sample size, and he's not a superstar.

But he's a good route runner, with great hands, and a smart head on his shoulders.

Something the Chiefs have been missing at wide receiver for the last couple of seasons.

The only question now is, just how good is Josh Downs?

It's hard to say, especially when he's had the likes of Anthony Richardson, Gardner Minshew, and Daniel Jones throwing him the football over the last four seasons.

Another thing that the jury is still out on is how much better this team is with Downs on it?

Maybe we already knew this one before this game.

But there is a difference between being the change-of-pace back and being the workhorse expected to put the offense on your shoulders and carry it downfield to the promised land.

With starting running back Kenneth Walker sidelined with injury, the Chiefs rookie running back got his first chance to be the first man up in the backfield, and boy howdy, he did not disappoint.

This Twitter post sells Johnson short. According to ESPN, Johnson finished the game with 101 rushing yards, 70 receiving yards, and two touchdowns.

I don't think there is any question that Johnson has been the best rookie running back in the NFL this season. It's not surprising, considering he was fourth in the nation in rushing last season while playing at Nebraska.

A lot will be made this week about the Chiefs rediscovering their deep ball.

But none of the above happens without Johnson keeping the defense honest.

We obviously want Kenneth Walker III back as soon as possible, but until then, we've got E-Money.

With the turmoil at the Chiefs' offensive tackle position this season, Kansas City has at least been able to rest on the fact that when left guard Kingsley Suamataia is not in head coach Andy Reid's doghouse, the Chiefs have one of the better interior offensive lines in the NFL.

So when right guard Trey Smith left the game with a concussion, the Chiefs had no choice but to put Mike Caliendo back in.

#2814. Rapid Recap: Hawks 113, Bucks 106

The Milwaukee Bucks led by as much as 22 at the start of the second quarter, blew the lead by halftime, and never retook the lead in Sunday's 113-106 loss to the Atlanta Hawks. The Bucks threatened to take the lead late in the fourth quarter, largely thanks to a career-high night from Kasparas Jakucionis, who scored 25 points (21 of which came in the second half). However, the Bucks struggled with turnovers and unnecessary fouling, which slowed their momentum and allowed the Hawks to hold on. Gary Trent Jr. scored 18 points, while Kyle Kuzma and Brayden Burries had 14 and 13, respectively. CJ McCollum and Nickeil Alexander-Walker each accounted for 20 points, while Onyeka Okongwu had 16 and Jalen Johnson added 14.

The Bucks started the game with the upper hand. Kyle Kuzma poured in the first three, splashing a bomb as the shot clock expired, and the Bucks kept the hot shooting going. Myles Turner added one, and Kuzma made two more triples within the first six minutes, prompting a quick timeout from Hawks head coach Quin Snyder. Milwaukee forced frequent Atlanta turnovers, kept them cold from the perimeter, and shot well on offense. Jaime Jaquez Jr. even scored a fading corner three to push the Bucks' lead to 13 points, and after Kel'el Ware swatted a Corey Kispert layup attempt off the glass, Gary Trent Jr. fired a transition bomb to give the Bucks a first-quarter lead of 16! Trent's fast-break layup with just over 36 seconds left gave Milwaukee a 37-18 lead by the end of the first quarter—their best quarter of the season by a country mile.

Milwaukee pressed its advantage to open the second quarter, with Trent and Jaquez connecting on early triples, but Atlanta slowly began to break out of its shooting slump. The Hawks embarked on an 11-3 run, led by an Alexander-Walker layup that trimmed Milwaukee's lead to just 13. RayJ Dennis found Onyeka Okongwu open in the paint with a slicing pass, and Okongwu dunked on Myles Turner to quiet the raucous Fiserv Forum. Jalen Johnson powered in a fast-break dunk, cutting the Bucks' lead to single digits, and the home team suddenly found itself on the back foot. Milwaukee's hot first-quarter shooting had failed them; they shot just 3/15 from three in the second quarter as the Hawks upped their defensive intensity. The Bucks' intensity, by contrast, seemed to wane as their lead shrank, giving up several offensive rebounds and turning the ball over on silly errors; Milwaukee totalled eight second-quarter turnovers. A pair of McCollum free throws tied the game with a little over a minute remaining, and a fadeaway from Alexander-Walker after lazy rebounding efforts gave them the lead; up 55-53 at half.

Atlanta extended its lead after the half. Dyson Daniels connected on a pull-up jumper, Alexander-Walker scored from the midrange, and NAW cashed a three, pushing Atlanta's edge to eight with nine minutes remaining in the third. The Bucks looked like they'd been punched in the mouth as Taylor Jenkins called the first timeout. However, the Hawks struggled to pull away; despite leading by ten points midway through the third, Milwaukee's defense tightened to cool Atlanta's hot shooting and continued to cut the lead. Milwaukee's shooting improved, especially from Jakucionis, who poured in 10 points in the third quarter. Daniels got a floater to go at the end of the third quarter, leaving Atlanta up 87-80 entering the final frame.

With Milwaukee's second win of the season on the line, they didn't let up. The Bucks stayed competitive, opening the final frame with a four-point play by Trent (yes, Gary Trent Jr.). The Bucks feasted on the Hawks' lack of ball security, cutting the deficit to as little as one, and they sought contact on both ends of the court, a welcome sign for a young, developing team. Jakucionis and Turner each had huge and-ones, the latter trimming Atlanta's lead to just two points with 6:32 left. However, Milwaukee got into foul trouble late, and Burries and Jakucionis missed crucial jumpers that kept the game close. Milwaukee, especially the frontcourt, was too handsy on defense and kept sending sharpshooters like McCollum and Johnson to the line. Jakucionis kept the Bucks in the game, though, spearheading fast-break opportunities and hitting some big jumpers down the line. His three-pointer with 1:57 remaining trimmed Atlanta's lead to three, up 109-106, and they remained in the game when Okongwu fumbled an easy fast-break layup. Jakucionis saw his heat-check three-pointer go in and out, and McCollum went down the other end and iced a step-back midrange jumper to give the Hawks a two-possession lead with just 17 seconds left, which they wouldn't relinquish.

The Hawks had 15 fourth-quarter free throws to the Bucks' nine. The Bucks, and especially Myles Turner, were undisciplined down the stretch when it came to foul discipline. Those needless fouls derailed Milwaukee's comeback bid and gave an otherwise struggling Hawks team a free source of points.

It's time to give Kasparas Jakucionis a starting spot. The second-year guard fired from all cylinders in the second half, and his career-high 25 points came with great efficiency (9/16 from the field and 4/10 from beyond the arc). The Bucks' starting guards also struggled on Sunday; Tyler Herro had just five points on 13 shots, and Burries was pretty mediocre. Jakucionis could be an enticing option for Jenkins to turn to in response to the backcourt's inconsistency.

#2813. Warren Buffett donates $5.5 billion, says kindness 'compounds' in Thanksgiving letter

Billionaire Warren Buffett donated Berkshire Hathaway shares worth about $5.5 billion to four philanthropic foundations, stepping up his annual charitable giving from the prior year.

Buffett, 96, converted 6,150 Class A Berkshire shares into 9,225,000 Class B shares and gave 3,690,000 of those shares to The Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, and 1,845,000 shares each to The Sherwood Foundation, The Howard G. Buffett Foundation and NoVo Foundation — the foundations run by his three children, Susie, Howard and Peter Buffett.

Following the donations, the chairman emeritus of Berkshire still owns 182,140 Class A shares — roughly $162 billion.

"Last Thanksgiving I wrote that kindness is costless but also priceless. A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie and I ever bought," wrote Buffett, who was succeeded as chairman in September by his son Howard, in his annual Thanksgiving message.

The letter was much shorter than his message last Thanksgiving. Charlie Munger, former vice chairman of Berkshire, passed away in November 2023.

Berkshire shares have been on a tear recently, up more than 19% since the start of the fourth quarter and ending last week with four consecutive record high closes. That compares with the S&P 500's decline of 1.2% so far for the fourth quarter. Berkshire has been seen as a safe haven as investors have worried about the artificial intelligence buildout, corporate drama and geopolitical risk.

The shares are now outperforming the S&P 500 for the year by about 7.5 percentage points.

In his letter, Buffett reiterated that all of his Berkshire shares eventually will be donated to the four foundations, with the goal being to increase the size of the grants each year.

Buffett wrote that the gifts were a signal of his confidence not only in his three children, but also in Berkshire CEO Greg Abel:

"My children have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity. ... You heard plenty from me in September about my confidence in Berkshire. That confidence is as strong as ever," he said.

Referring to Munger and himself, Buffett added, "Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received."

"Warren continues to share his wisdom with us all during this holiday season. His example reflects the kindness he encourages in all of us," said CEO Abel of the letter.

Buffett closes his message with this advice: "This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

#2812. The Good, Bad & Ugly from the Miami Dolphins Week 10 loss to the Indianapolis Colts

The Miami Dolphins were extremely shorthanded heading into their Week 10 road-trip against the Indianapolis Colts. Starting quarterback Malik Willis was placed on injured reserve earlier in the week with a lingering groin issue. Stud center Aaron Brewer missed Sunday's tilt due a concussion he suffered in last week's loss to the Detroit Lions. Defensive leader Zach Sieler tore his biceps in that same loss — forcing him to not only miss Miami's game against Indianapolis, but the rest of the 2026 season as well.

Well, as we all know, adversity is opportunity.

The Dolphins — while starting rookie quarterback Kyle McCord — nearly overcame all of that adversity to upset their foes on Sunday. Instead, despite a spirited effort from Jeff Hafley's squad, Miami lost by two-points against Indianapolis, dropping their record to 1-8 on the year after falling by a score of 24-22.

In a season where finding bright spots has sometimes felt like searching for a needle in a haystack, seeing two young players make game-changing defensive plays for Miami on Sunday was encouraging.

Rookie safety Michael Taaffe and rookie cornerback Chris Johnson each came away with an interception of Colts' quarterback Daniel Jones.

Taaffe got things started with an interception with 10:30 left in the second quarter — giving Miami's defense a much-needed takeaway while the Colts were driving for points. It was Taaffe's third interception on the season. Not bad for a 5th round pick!

But it was Johnson who provided the highlight of the afternoon.

Miami's 1st round rookie cornerback intercepted Jones with 8:52 left in the 3rd-quarter and returned the ball 59 yards for a dazzling touchdown — giving Miami a big lift and injecting some life into a game that was quickly getting away from the South Florida squad.

Obviously, one game doesn't make a career, but Taaffe and Johnson have been playing solid football for the Dolphins all season long. There hasn't been a whole lot to celebrate in Miami this year, but seeing two rookies stepping up when their team needs them is part of the fuel that keeps the fires of hope burning as Dolphins fans look towards a brighter future.

Now, if only the rest of the defense could figure out how to stop somebody…

On Sunday, Indianapolis ran the football 34 times for 172 yards, averaging a whopping 5.1 yards per carry.

The Colts didn't need to get particularly creative. They didn't need to rely on some elaborate offensive scheme or trick plays. They lined up, handed the football to their running backs Jonathan Taylor and Seth McGowan, and repeatedly found themselves moving the chains.

The Indianapolis rushing attack consistently found room to operate, putting Miami's defense on their heels and making life difficult for the Dolphins throughout the afternoon, despite the fact that Miami's defense only gave up 104 yards passing.

When an opposing offense can consistently pick up five yards every time it hands the ball off, you're eventually going to run out of opportunities to get off the field.

The Dolphins have plenty of things to figure out defensively, but stopping the run needs to be near the top of that list — especially when a date with Josh Allen, James Cook and the Buffalo Bills is circled on the calendar for next Sunday.

Zane Gonzalez misses two field goals and an extra-point try in a two-point loss

When the Dolphins gave kicker Riley Patterson his walking papers on October 20th so they could sign Zane Gonzalez to take over kicking duties for the team, fans were encouraged by the move.

Fast forward four games and that warm and fuzzy feeling has vanished in a hurry.

On Sunday, Gonzalez missed two field goals and an extra point try. That's 7 points Gonzalez was unable to put on the board for a Dolphins team desperate for them.

Reminder: Miami lost Sunday's game by two points.

Gonzalez's first missed opportunity came on a 47-yard attempt in the first quarter. Later, after Miami had clawed their way back into the game, he missed the extra point try following Chris Johnson's spectacular interception return for a touchdown.

However, Miami's kicker saved his most heart-breaking moment for the fourth quarter when he missed a long try that would have given the Dolphins the lead in a tight, back-and-forth contest.

As I gaze into my crystal ball, I predict the Dolphins to look elsewhere for a place kicker in very short order.

The Dolphins, with their backs against the wall, played a close game against the Colts on Sunday. Alas, they fell short — dropping the team's record to 1-8 on the year. Were you impressed with quarterback Kyle McCord's first start? Let me know in the comments below or on Twitter at @MBrave13. Fins up.
