#2808. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2807. Nvidia fallout: Commerce cracks down on third-country loophole that allowed China to use computing power

The Commerce Department moved on Monday to close a loophole that has let Chinese companies lease Nvidia's advanced computing power from data centers in third countries, effectively circumventing controls on sensitive chip technology being exported to China.

Commerce's action, an interim final rule issued by its Bureau of Industry and Security, is the first U.S. policy response to the scandal at Nvidia that led CEO Jensen Huang to resign last week.

The department, in a press release, said that the BIS rule was "written by and at the direction of" the Treasury Department and the National Security Council.

The rule came after rising criticism of Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has been accused of not doing enough to prevent possible diversion of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China in violation of American export controls.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, in an interview on Friday with CNBC's "Squawk Box," blamed the Commerce Department for not putting Megaspeed, the Singapore-based company at the center the Nvidia scandal, on the so-called entity list, which would restrict certain exports to it.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who, as national security advisor, leads the National Security Council, warned President Donald Trump last spring that Huang was in a close relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence believed to be a Chinese spy, The New York Times reported on Nov. 8. Jensen Huang, who is not related to Alice, resigned last week after other reports said he had affairs with her and a subordinate at Nvidia.

The Commerce Department, in the notifice of the IFR on Monday, said that it "is establishing new rules to prevent certain foreign actors from leveraging advanced computing power based on U.S. technology to threaten U.S. national security and foreign policy interests."

These restrictions will undermine the ability of companies headquartered in countries of concern from using U.S. advanced Super Intelligence (SI) semiconductors to train advanced SI models," the rule said.

The rule also says that a license will now "be required to export, reexport, or transfer (in-country) advanced semiconductors to Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil (and any additional country designated by the Secretary of Commerce, in consultation with the Secretary of State or the Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs) – countries which present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion."

The rule was published a day after Sen. Maria Cantwell of Washington state, the ranking Democrat on the Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation, in a letter to Lutnick said that Chinese companies such as Alibaba and ByteDance have "used overseas data centers equipped with advanced Nvidia chips to train advanced AI models."

"These companies are exploiting a loophole in U.S. export controls by using advanced American chips in foreign data centers even when those chips are restricted from the [People's Republic of China]," Cantwell wrote.

"Despite warnings from the Bureau of Industry and Security that certain arrangements may violate existing restrictions, the Department of Commerce failed to close this loophole, allowing PRC companies to advance their AI capabilities using sensitive American technology."

#2806. JJ Redick gets real on Lakers ‘ceiling,’ frustrating blown leads

Before the season began, he raised eyebrows with a bold claim: he was "confident" the Lakers could become "a championship-level team this year."

They were miles behind the Thunder and Spurs in the Western Conference.

But 14 games into the season, Redick is the one chuckling at his detractors after the Lakers won eight games in a row and are tied for the best record in the league at 12-2.

Some of the wins haven't been pretty, including Sunday's 131-123 win over the Suns in which they watched a 31-point lead disintegrate to five points in the fourth quarter before Quentin Grimes banked in a 3-pointer and Luka Doncic made a pair of free throws to save the day.

Despite Lakers coach JJ Redick having his analytics department prepare a 21-page audit detailing where LA can improve offensively and defensively, it's clear the team has wildly surpassed expectations.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick looks on during the first quarter against the Milwaukee Bucks at Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. Getty Images

And Redick thinks this is just the beginning.

"I don't think we should put a ceiling on this group," Redick said. "This group is committed to getting better every single day, recognizing that this is a process to become a high-level team. And that's what I want from this group, is hold each other to that standard every single time. And that's the frustrating part at times because you see how good we can be. There's no reason to ever play less than that standard, you know?"

Redick is like the never-satisfied father who sees the potential in his son. He rides him. He pushes him.

That style is working with this Lakers team.

Luka Doncic is playing MVP-level basketball, including finishing with his seventh 40-point performance in 12 games on Sunday, with 43 points, three rebounds and five assists.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Walker Kessler had his fifth straight double-double against the Suns with 14 points and 12 rebounds. And Austin Reaves, who was sidelined Sunday because of a bruised heel, looks like the best No. 2 in the league.

They also have some things going wrong: When Doncic isn't on the floor, the Lakers blow leads. Their third unit can't be trusted. And defensively, they're ranked No. 18.

But they're the top-rated team in the league on offense and they entered Sunday's game leading the league in scoring (122.2 points per game), field goal percentage (50.3%), steals (11 per game) and points off turnovers (24.5 per game).

Walker Kessler of the Los Angeles Lakers looks on during a game against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Redick is right not to put a ceiling on the Lakers.

He got them to buy into their championship potential.

And now he's pushing them to believe they haven't even tapped into who they could become despite being atop the league.

"Honestly, I think we have a lot of space for improvement," Doncic said. "Obviously one thing for now is blowing some leads. I think we got to work on that, for sure. But I think every aspect of the game, I feel like we have a lot of improvement left."

The Lakers blew a 25-point lead to the Warriors in a 112-95 loss on Oct. 30. At least Sunday, they pumped the brakes and recovered. The Lakers are still figuring things out.

#2805. Falcons – Chiefs recap: Wilting away in Mercedes-Benz Stadium

If you were daydreaming about an improbable run, simply hoping for a miracle, or just looking for something enjoyable as the season very slowly winds down, the Falcons were determined to ensure your Sunday left you bereft. The quality of the opponent aside—the Chiefs are very good, obviously—this was the bottom for the Falcons, and the point at which team and fanbase alike need to fully accept certain hard truths about the Atlanta Falcons of 2026.

Those truths? The first and most obvious one is that this is a bad football team at the moment. Despite some promising signings and trades, the talent level is not good enough across the board to make them a playoff-caliber team even in a weak NFC South, something the Falcons appeared to accept when they traded away Jessie Bates and Ryan Neuzil. Also, while this team has more resources than just any other team in the NFL, they are going to need every dollar and draft pick just to overhaul what they have in a way that brings their talent level up to where it needs to be and exorcises all the weird ghosts that have been awkwardly floating around this team for most of the last decade. And finally, there are very few positions on this roster that are actually settled with 2027 looming, meaning this team could look very, very different than it does now by May of next year.

There is a Sunday every year in this miserable stretch of losing seasons where the terrible weight of losing lands especially heavy on my shoulders, and this is the one for 2026. More than likely, I'll come out of the bye ready to joke about the Falcons reeling off some unlikely wins and finishing this year with a losing record for the ninth straight season, and we'll all lock arms and head into a pivotal offseason daring to dream of something better. But I'll tell you with naked honesty that this Sunday got to me in a way the previous nine games did not, as I saw the this team continue a frankly brutal three game slide and make it fully clear just how limited their ceiling for this season really is. At 3-7 and driving that stake into this team's hopes with relish, the Falcons ensured I can fully give up on 2026 and just try to find the bright spots in what's to come, and I do intend to take full advantage. I hope you will, as will.

For now, though, we'll be digesting a game where Patrick Mahomes threw for five touchdowns and the Chiefs ran all over Atlanta despite not having Kenneth Walker, and the defense was still the more impressive unit. Atlanta's offense has now scored 17 or fewer points in three straight games, which begs the question of whether Tommy Rees is the right coordinator, Penix is the right quarterback, and almost every player on that side of the ball is going to be part of the next great Falcons offense outside of Bijan Robinson, Chris Lindstrom, and Drake London. That seems like an overreaction to one game—and perhaps even three games—but this is an evaluation year for a regime with the resources to make substantial changes in 2027, and an offense studded with talented players looking this crummy will give them genuine pause. The Chiefs are a good, well-coached team, but the gulf between them and a frankly hapless Falcons team should give them further pause.

The bye week will give these Falcons a chance to reset and try to make something of the rest of this year, but the fate of this team seems beyond obvious at this point. As we have so many times over the past decade, we Falcons fans are left to look ahead to better days after the last bloom has fallen off the rose. May this coming offseason finally bring those long-elusive days to us, so that we may put games like in the past where they belong.

He hasn't looked superhuman very often lately, but Bijan Robinson remains a player defenses fear and a back capable of breaking some jaw-dropping runs. His best carries in this game were like that, where through sheer will, balance, and power he managed to reel off 10-plus yards despite the at-times nonexistent threat of the Falcons passing game working against him. Someday the offense will sputter to life again and he'll look like the Bijan of old.

Jahan Dotson would ideally be your third receiver, but even with his ups and downs he's been a very credible receiving threat for this team, and has had some really good weeks. He was a better pass away from a touchdown today and finished second on the team with three receptions for 63 yards, and is on his way to a career year in an offense that can and wants to get him the ball.

Drake London deserves a medal of some kind. His career has been defined by a revolving door of play callers, quarterbacks, and surrounding personnel, and yet he continually blocks his ass off and shows out as the team's best receiver. He somehow had over 100 yards in this one despite the passing game being dormant much of the day, and London deserves better than the results this team sees in the win and loss columns weekly.

Harold Perkins had a couple of ugly reps on the first defensive drive, and that must have galvanized him because he was a man possessed after that point. Perkins was fantastic against the run, a demon as a pass rusher, and more stingy in coverage after that point, standing out over and over again no matter what he was asked to do. There's little question Perkins is a player the Falcons plan to count on in 2027 and beyond, and he showed why on Sunday.

Brandon Dorlus and Gervon Dexter remain one of the best duos on the interior in the NFL, and Dexter in particular was a man possessed with five pressures, three run stops, and multiple plays where he rampaged through the overmatched Chiefs offensive line like Godzilla through the financial district. If the Falcons can bring more consistent pressure off the edge to help out D&D, quarterbacks will be miserable.

The defense did the heavy lifting on Sunday against a good Chiefs team minus key pieces, and reminded us they're the unit that is much closer to being a finished product, even if the overall results were still rough. It's not that difficult to talk yourself into Billy Bowman Jr., Xavier Watts, Kendal Daniels, Perkins, Dorlus, Dexter, and company just getting better a year from now with better complementary pieces around them.

Jake Moody came uncomfortably close to missing an extra point, but otherwise he was excellent. Moody hit both short field goal tries and had the NFL's first successful onside kick attempt, with a major assist from Chris Blair, in his Falcons debut. Hopefully he can prove to be the team's long-term solution at kicker, and the early returns were promising, even if the kicks weren't particularly challenging.

Blair, as mentioned above, had a phenomenal play. He wasn't quite there on a potential touchdown pass from Penix, but the onsides kick recovery was flawless, as Blair very confidently grabbed it away from a sleepwalking Xavier Worthy and kept his feet inbounds to give Atlanta a last gasp chance they couldn't take full advantage of.

Yes, the offense has holes and weaknesses that need to be addressed in the offseason, but it's not so bereft of talent that it should look like it did on the first four drives. The Falcons started this game with one productive Bijan Robinson carry, one eight yard catch for Kyle Pitts, and a whole lot of incompletions, penalties, and ineptitude that led to four straight three-and-outs. That represented all four drives in the first quarter, and was evidence enough that this team selling at the deadline was an accurate read of their potential. It was, as the broadcast noted, the first time since 2000 that the Falcons started the game with four straight three-and-outs.

Michael Penix Jr. simply can't miss this consistently if this offense is going to be anything of note. On the first pass of the game he had an open Drake London and missed him, and his misfires piled up throughout the first half as Atlanta continually frittered away their drives. Matt Ryan praised Penix before the game but made it clear the Falcons were looking for more consistency from him, and consistency continues to elude the third-year quarterback, given that this was another forgettable day from a player who continues to look like he needs ideal situations to thrive. If that continues, it's unlikely he'll be the starter in September 2027.

Kyle Pitts has become something of an avatar for this offense. Both are highly regarded for their potential, capable of absolutely eye-popping weeks, and yet prone to inconsistency and dreary performances. Pitts had a solid four catches for 50 yards, but that's not outstanding production, he wasn't a huge factor after the catch, and he had one catch stripped for a Chiefs turnover. A player who seemed destined to thrive in Kevin Stefanski and Tommy Rees' offense just hasn't.

Jawaan Taylor was called out during the broadcast for giving away the play call with his stance and then had multiple false start calls. The Falcons will be in the market for a new right tackle in 2027.

Jake Matthews was hit with three separate penalties, and the legendary left tackle appears to be closing in on the end of the line. Did the Falcons want to have to replace both tackles in 2027? They'll chalk this up as an off day for Matthews, who was pretty solid outside of those penalties, but Father Time is tapping his foot and Bill Callahan seems likely to put his stamp on more than just the center position.

The Falcons defense was by far the better unit through the first half, but they still had brutal lapses that gave the Chiefs a 14-3 lead. The worst single play was on Emmett Johnson's touchdown grab in the first quarter, which saw two Falcons get a shot at bringing Johnson down and utterly failing to do so, as missed tackles and bad angles led to both Kansas City scores. It's hard to hold it against them too much given that they were on the field nearly constantly, but miscues are miscues, and Atlanta had tons of them.

The run defense is a five alarm fire at this point. Johnson is a promising backup for Kansas City, but he is no Walker, yet his production suggested he was possessed by the veteran back's spirit. Since dominating some lousy ground games early on in the year, the Falcons have been among the least inspiring defenses in football and are both struggling to get into position and struggling to tackle opposing backs for going on six weeks now.

This was a very, very tough game for Atlanta's secondary, which Mahomes picked apart with his usual precision. A.J. Terrell allowed three receptions for 86 yards and two touchdowns, C.J. Henderson gave up three more for 97 yards and a score, and Billy Bowman Jr.'s mostly strong day was undone by three grabs for 41 yards and another score. Terrell in particular has been brutal the past two weeks, and the Falcons have to figure something out in the back end before Kirk Cousins tries out his spin on a revenge tour in two weeks.

The Falcons had Tommy Rees down on the sidelines, which is usually a sign that the offense is struggling (it is) and the team is taking a hard look at their coordinator's fortunes (to be determined), and the offense put up another dud of a performance. Cooper Rush being the quarterback badly hurt the Falcons the first two weeks, but they've already turned the ball over on offense more than they did in 2025 and now have scored 17 or fewer points in five of their ten games and three straight weeks. There are so many execution errors and penalty issues that you can't lay this all at Rees' feet, but the flashes of really thrilling offense just aren't there right now, with a strangled run game, too many early passes, and go-nowhere screens damaging the effort.

It's a bad sign that this team is continually getting worse as the season rolls on. You'd expect Michael Penix Jr. to be playing better football than he was fresh off a long injured stint early in the year, but he's not. You'd think this defense would be starting to figure things out with so many young players in the lineup, but they're getting destroyed by quality offenses. This coaching staff is not going anywhere, as I've said repeatedly, but it would be extremely unideal for them to go into a pivotal 2027 with any shadows of doubt hanging over them. Right now, it's hard not to feel like this staff is failing to get more out of a team that looks incredibly sloppy and penalty-prone, at the very least.

This is a team that's not even in the same stratosphere as real contenders in the NFL. They're not even in the same breath as the teams they're nominally just behind in the NFC, frankly.

A bye week. We'll take stock of what has passed and what's to come, and then get ready for the Vikings.

#2804. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2803. Nets’ jumbo lineup proving to be intriguing wrinkle — even with mixed results

It was deadly in New Orleans. It misfired the next game in Memphis — albeit with lesser ammo. But the Nets have it all the same.

Coach Jordi Fernández will likely use his historically big lineup less as Brooklyn's young guards eventually command more playing time. But he has the supersized wrinkle in his arsenal, ready to go to whenever he needs.

"Yeah, I think it helps," Fernández told The Post. "I mean, you think about Danny [Wolf] at the quote-unquote two, but obviously his versatility of shooting, being able to playmake, also a big body defensively that can rebound: All those things are important. Let's see how we keep evolving."

In each of the past two games, the Nets started five players all 6-foot-9 or taller.

No NBA team had started such a lineup in 37 seasons, when the Lakers fielded A.C. Green (6-foot-9), Magic Johnson (6-9), Mychal Thompson (6-10), Orlando Woolridge (6-9) and James Worthy (6-9) back on April 21, 1990.

Danny Wolf #2 of the Brooklyn Nets dribbles during a game against the Memphis Grizzlies at FedExForum in Memphis, Tenn., Saturday, Nov. 14, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

For Brooklyn, it provided intriguing — albeit mixed — results.

"It just speaks to the type of players we have," center Day'Ron Sharpe told The Post. "For us to have five 6-9 or higher on the court, speaks to the ability to switch one through five or switch one through four. And it gives a size for rebounding, which has been a big key for us.

"So even if I'm out there on the perimeter, you've got Danny, you've got Michael [Porter Jr.], you've got Julius [Randle], you've got Egor [Dëmin], who all could come back here and rebound. We've just got to keep buying into it."

In the past two games the Nets started Sharpe, Randle, Dëmin and Wolf, the latter's rare combo of size and ballhandling making him a 6-11 off-guard. They split the games, winning in New Orleans and falling Saturday in Memphis. The difference was leading scorer Porter missing the Grizzlies tilt, replaced in the starting lineup by Noah Clowney.

"Yeah, last year, we started off very small. Then, we transitioned over time to a bigger lineup and more length," Porter, who'll be back Monday vs. the 76ers, told The Post.

Julius Randle (30) of the Brooklyn Nets looks on during a game against the Memphis Grizzlies at FedExForum in Memphis, Tenn., Saturday, Nov. 14, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"It just helps out for sure in different aspects of the game. Danny's a great piece to connect players; he can rebound, he can push the ball, he can dribble the ball and just facilitate, he has a high IQ. So he's helped on the boards as well. And obviously having Egor back was big for us."

With the 6-foot-9 Dëmin returning from a bone bruise — and 6-foot-4 pure point guards Nolan Traore (sore calf) and Mikel Brown Jr. (illness) sidelined against New Orleans — the supersize starting lineup powered a 145-111 victory.

The starters logged a dozen minutes together and were a plus-18. All scored in double figures and were at least a plus-17, led by Randle's plus-38 — tied for the best in Nets history for a road game.

Michael Porter Jr., left, Terance Mann and Moritz Wagner react during a game against the New Orleans Pelicans on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, in New Orleans. Getty Images

But with Porter out against the Grizzlies, Fernández subbed Clowney in his place. That version was nowhere near as effective in a 129-110 loss, the Jumbo Lineup a minus-2 in 13 minutes. Every starter was a minus-7 or worse, except Sharpe (plus-4).

Granted, the competition was a lot tougher, the Pelicans 4-10 and losers of six straight while the Grizzlies are 9-4. Just as important was Porter, whose absence is expected to just be for one game.

Fernández himself has admitted Dëmin is best deployed off the ball, which means he'd eventually go back to two-guard and relegate Wolf back to the bench. The point guard spot would then be manned by either Traore or eventually Brown, the sixth overall pick and heir apparent once he's off his current minutes restriction.

But with Porter back, the Jumbo Lineup remains dangerous.

"Yeah, for sure. Obviously, it's a unique lineup," Wolf said. "And there's definitely some things that I've done well and a lot of things I can continue to work on.

"It also gives us a unique look defensively and on the glass. We've just got to continue to rebound at a high level with so much size out there."

→ Baca artikel asli

#2802. Grades: Lions offer hope despite Hutchinson injury

There was plenty to love about the Detroit Lions' dominant win over the New England Patriots. And while Aidan Hutchinson's injury may be the biggest headline from the Munich game, there was plenty from Allianz Arena that should keep Lions fans optimistic about the team moving forward.

Here is my Lions Week 10 report card and positional grades for Detroit's 35-10 victory.

Jared Goff threw two interceptions; both were his fault, and the second was an inexcusable decision that took points directly off the board. Because of those two plays alone, it feels like I can't give him a grade above C+ in good faith.

But the truth is the rest of his day was pretty dang good. I thought he navigated the pocket well, especially on his 50-yard pass to Jahmyr Gibbs. He didn't make a ton of downfield throws or display any jaw-dropping plays, but he was very efficient, finishing with an electric 9.8 yards per attempt and a 101.9 passer rating.

Jahmyr Gibbs was incredible all freakin' day. He finished with 252 scrimmage yards and two touchdowns. The Patriots couldn't handle his speed, his change-of-direction, or his strength. What was even better about his performance than normal is that the negative plays were almost completely non-existent. He had just one touch that went for negative yards, and only a couple more were stopped for zero yards.

Sione Vaki was very efficient, too, earning 28 yards on just six carries (4.7 YPC).

Overall, the Lions produced an impressive 55% success rate and +0.21 EPA/play on running plays vs. the Patriots. For comparison's sake, the Patriots were allowing just a 36.9 success rate going into this game and had produced a negative EPA/run.

I was a little disappointed with Sam LaPorta's play as a receiver. He didn't create much separation against the Patriots' defenders, and he had a touchdown grab knocked out of his hands. He finished with just a single catch for 16 yards on four targets.

However, the Lions used a ton of tight ends against the Patriots in the running game, and that played a significant role in Detroit controlling the line of scrimmage. LaPorta (26), Brock Wright (29), and Tyler Conklin (17) all logged over 15 run-blocking snaps on the day.

Amon-Ra St. Brown was seemingly unstoppable on Sunday. It was going to take the entire country of Germany to stop him on both of his long catch-and-run touchdowns, and they certainly weren't going to do that against their most beloved NFL player.

Jameson Williams' day shouldn't be overlooked, either. He caught four passes for 50 yards, including an impressive toe-tapping catch on the sidelines. Even Isaac TeSlaa got in on the fun with a highlight-reel touchdown grab.

This unit lived up to its full potential for the first time in several weeks.

Outside of some early pass protection issues, the Lions' offensive line was nails in Munich. They were getting significant push in the run game, and after settling down, they helped Jared Goff breathe comfortably in the pocket.

When the offensive line plays this well, the Lions' entire offense is as close to unstoppable as we've seen.

While the Lions' pressure was inconsistent at times without Aidan Hutchinson in the lineup, they still gave the Patriots hell for most of the game. In the run game, the Patriots were unable to do anything. Their running backs combined for 17 carries, 31 yards, and just 1.8 yards per carry.

The pass rush generated five total sacks, six quarterback hits, and over a 50 percent pressure rate:

It will be tough to maintain that without Hutchinson and against a better offensive line, but Derrick Moore, Alim McNeill, DJ Wonnum, and Skyler Gill-Howard have all played some pretty sound football, and all were disruptive on Sunday.

The linebackers deserve a lot of praise for Detroit's stout run defense, but they were a bit of a liability in the pass game again. It didn't end up costing the Lions too much in this game, because once Drake Maye went down, it looked like Tommy DeVito just didn't see the field very well. But coverage against tight ends and running backs continues to be a bit of an issue.

Still, this defense continues to play good football without its field general at MIKE linebacker, and that means something.

I thought the safety play was fantastic on Sunday. Brian Branch was all over the place in the run game and the pass game. Chuck Clark flipped a couple Patriots and made the receiving corps pay for every DeVito murderball.

I can't give them an A, though, because of a couple of stupid and costly pass interference flags. It wasn't a great game for Ennis Rakestraw—who also missed a key tackle—nor Nick Whiteside. But the good news is the top corners in this game—Rock Ya-Sin and Roger McCreary were very, very good. Or, as Dan Campbell said:

"I thought the back end played outstanding," Campbell said. "We played some sticky coverage today, bought us a little bit of time."

Nothing too notable from this group on Sunday. The return team didn't look particularly good, but coverage was good on both punt and kickoff. Jake Bates is still perfect when Jack Fox isn't blocking his kicks. He did put a kickoff out of bounds and a touchback in the end zone.

This was a tough week for both coaches. Flying across the globe, adjusting to a time zone six hours away, and dealing with all of the crazy logistics of getting ready for Sunday is not easy. It's clear one team was way more prepared than the other.

On top of that, Lions offensive coordinator Drew Petzing is really starting to come into his own, building plays off of previous plays, and seemingly having the right call in the right moment more often than not. As he hits his stride, the Lions offense can carry them a long way.

But don't sleep on the defensive coaching, either. Kelvin Sheppard has had to deal with two emotionally draining injuries to two of their best players. Yet through a heavy rotation at every level of the defense, things have never looked more steady and controlled. Say what you will, facing a beat-up Patriots offense with a backup quarterback leading most of Sunday afternoon, but Detroit used to get beat up by backups all the time. The defense finally feels like it's coming together.

#2797. Lakers guard mimics Paul Pierce’s iconic line after burying Suns

PHOENIX — Quentin Grimes broke out of his early-season shooting slump in a major way during the Lakers' 131-123 win over the Suns on Sunday at Mortgage Matchup Center.

And he arguably had the biggest sequence of the game to seal the victory.

Quentin Grimes of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

After the Suns cut what was once a 31-point Lakers lead late in the third quarter to five points with just over a minute left in the game, Jake LaRavia missed a corner 3-pointer that could've given the Lakers a larger cushion or given the Suns an opportunity to make it a single-possession game.

Grimes made sure the latter didn't happen, crashing the offensive glass and grabbing the rebound off LaRavia's miss to give the Lakers another shot at scoring.

Quentin Grimes (5) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles the ball against the Phoenix Suns on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026, at Mortgage Matchup Center in Phoenix. NBAE via Getty Images

The veteran wing took matters into his own hands when he banked in a pull-up 3 to put the Lakers up 129-121 with 32.2 seconds left to ice the game.

Did Grimes call bank before making the game-sealing 3?

"I called game," Grimes said before repeating the line twice. "I mean, the bank ain't open on Sunday. I called game. Trying to get out of here and get back to L.A."

Paul Pierce, who was playing for the Wizards at the time, famously responded "I called game!" after banking in the game-winning shot of Game 3 of the Wizards' Eastern Conference Semifinals playoff series against the Hawks.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Quentin Grimes speaks to reporters following a game against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

While Grimes had fun with his response to whether he intentionally banked in the 3, Sunday was an important step for him to break out of his shooting slump.

The sixth-year guard made a season-high five 3s against the Suns, with his performance on Sunday increasing his 3-point percentage from 24.2% to 33.3%.

The Lakers are confident Grimes, who's shot just over 36% on 3s for his career, will continue to find his groove with his perimeter shot.

"Watching him every day, when he is in the gym, he's very consistent with his routine," coach JJ Redick said. "And we tell players this all the time: Whether you're shooting well or you're shooting poorly, you should just keep the same routine. Because otherwise, then it becomes, 'is shooting a superstitious thing?' I don't think it is, right? Shooting is based on form and reps and confidence, and the only way to build that confidence is through the reps.

"He's just handled it really well. He's been in the gym, he's got the shots up, he's done his routine. He's historically had some really good shooting seasons both from catch-and-shoot and off-the-dribble. We don't think he's a 26% [3-point shooter] or whatever he is, I don't know what he's been before [Sunday] but somewhere in the 20s. He's a high 30s guy."

#2791. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2790. Maradona jr su Napoli-Lazio: «i ritiri non servono veramente a nulla»

Nel giudizio dell’ex calciatore pesano l’approccio del Napoli e le ripartenze concesse ai biancocelesti nel primo tempo.

Dopo l’1-1 tra Napoli e Lazio, Diego Armando Maradona junior ha criticato l’avvio degli azzurri e messo in discussione l’utilità dei ritiri. Intervenuto ai microfoni di Tele A, l’ex calciatore ha rivolto un giudizio severo soprattutto al primo tempo, soffermandosi sull’approccio alla gara e sulle ripartenze concesse ai biancocelesti.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Maradona junior ha descritto così l’inizio della partita: «Credo che i primi 15 minuti del Napoli contro la Lazio siano stati terrificanti. La Lazio ci ha messo seriamente alle corde. Poi pian piano il fatto di non prendere gol ci ha dato fiducia e abbiamo iniziato a giochicchiare».

Nel suo giudizio, dunque, la Lazio ha messo in difficoltà il Napoli fin dalle prime battute. La squadra azzurra ha evitato di subire gol in quella fase e, secondo Maradona junior, ha trovato gradualmente più fiducia.

L’ex calciatore ha distinto l’avvio, definito terrificante, dai minuti successivi. Pur riconoscendo che il Napoli abbia cominciato a giocare dopo aver resistito alla pressione iniziale, ha scelto il verbo giochicchiare per descrivere quella fase della prestazione.

#2788. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2786. Raiders TE Brock Bowers leaves loss to Seahawks early with ankle injury

Brock Bowers didn't last long on Sunday afternoon.

The Las Vegas Raiders tight end was ruled out of their 21-13 loss to the Seattle Seahawks due to an ankle injury he suffered early in the first quarter. That knocked him out for the majority of Fernando Mendoza's starting debut at Allegiant Stadium.

Bowers, in the first quarter of the contest, went up to block Seahawks safety Nick Emmanwori for running back Ashton Jeanty. But as Jeanty cut the other way, Bowers had his legs rolled up on in a pile and he went down awkwardly.

Bowers remained down for a few moments while the team's training staff helped him, and he then limped to the sideline.

Bowers did return to the game briefly after that, which helped lead the way for Mendoza's rushing touchdown to get them on the board. But he left the game again late in the second quarter, and the team ruled him out for good just before the start of the second half with the ankle injury. Clearly, it was just too much for him to overcome.

Bowers may have escaped the contest without a major injury, per NFL Network. Early reports suggest the tight end is more day-to-day with his ankle issue. He'll undergo tests Monday to determine the severity of the injury.

Bowers finished with two catches for 18 yards. He entered Sunday with 658 yards and six touchdowns this season, his third in the league after the Raiders took him with the No. 13 overall pick out of Georgia in 2024.

Though the Raiders entered the locker room up by a field goal, the Seahawks scored a pair of touchdowns in the third quarter to break the game open. Jadarian Price ran it in from 12 yards out first, and then Jaxon Smith-Njigba hauled in a 71-yard pass from Drew Lock to give Seattle an 11-point lead at the time.

That was enough to secure the eight-point win.

Lock ended up going 14-of-17 for 240 yards with two touchdowns in the win for Seattle, which improved to 6-4 on the season. Zach Charbonnet had 94 rushing yards on two carries, and Smith-Njigba had 171 yards and a score on eight catches.

The Raiders dealt away quarterback Kirk Cousins at the deadline, which paved the way for Mendoza, the former Indiana star and reigning Heisman Trophy winner, to get the first start of his career. Mendoza went 17-of-29 for 143 yards in the loss. Mike Washington led them on the ground with 23 yards, and Jeanty had 46 receiving yards on four catches. The Raiders now sit at 5-5 on the year.

Though Mendoza appears to be holding his own so far, losing a target like Bowers for any substantial amount of time will make things much more difficult for him as his NFL career gets going.

#2785. James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays ‘before they actually happen'

James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays 'before they actually happen' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Tyrese Maxey admitted that he wasn't entirely ready for LeBron James' first pass of the afternoon Sunday.

Maxey cut through the paint and James' bounce pass was perfect, but the Sixers' All-NBA guard missed his layup. He's not fully accustomed yet to James' precognitive gifts.

"There's still some times when you're surprised," Maxey said after the Sixers' win over the Pacers. "… I was cutting through just to cut and create space. The ball hit me in the chest and I missed the layup. I was like, 'Ah shoot, my bad.' He was like, 'Yeah, you weren't looking.'"

James has been trying to teach his teammates that he'll spot openings they may not notice.

"I just tell my guys, 'Always pay attention to me when I've got the ball,' because I definitely see plays develop before they happen," James said.

For the most part, James' teammates adhered to his request on the latest historic day in a milestone-filled career. By setting up VJ Edgecombe's layup with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter, James moved into third place on the NBA's all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd.

The 22-time All-Star recorded 11 assists, his most so far as a Sixer, and now sits at 12,092 in his career. Chris Paul (12,552 assists) and John Stockton (15,806) are the only two players above James.

"It's obviously super humbling," James said. "It's something that I didn't foresee in my career, but I knew assists meant a lot to me. Just the movement of the ball, being able to find my teammates, putting the ball on time and on target.

"And also just being linked with some of the greats that have ever played this game and a dear friend of mine, J-Kidd. It's really special."

James is at or near the top of many all-time leaderboards. He's first in NBA history in points, games and minutes, sixth in steals and fifth in triple-doubles.

Passing has always been a core element of his game.

"First of all, he's got the vision and the awareness," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He's got the size to see. I think he's got some anticipation. Just a half-second before a play happens, he knows how the defense is moving. … And he throws crisp passes. Some of those are cooking out to the corner three guys, so it doesn't let the close-out get there nearly as quickly."

Whether it's a long-range rocket, a drive-and-kick dish or a cross-court feed from the post that suggests he might have eyes in the back of his head, James has always enjoyed finding a wide range of ways to help his teammates.

"For someone that talented, he's very unselfish," Maxey said. "He really prides himself on getting guys open."

As James recalled Sunday, he had exceptional passing tools long before he entered the NBA 24 seasons ago.

"Since I was a kid, I've had the ability to see plays before they actually happen," he said. "Seeing movement, seeing cracks in the defense before they were even open … it's something that's been with me since I was a kid.

"I just had that vision and it just stuck with me throughout my playing days."

#2784. Drake Maye reportedly could miss remainder of season with 'significant lower leg injury,' Pats fear ankle fracture

New England Patriots quarterback Drake Maye reportedly could potentially miss the remainder of the season with what is described as a "significant lower-leg injury," NFL Network reported early Monday.

The Patriots reportedly fear Maye could be dealing with a fractured ankle, per ESPN.

Maye did not travel with the team to the United States after the contest, instead staying in Germany to undergo additional testing. The injury could require surgery and Maye is looking at a lengthy absence, if not a season-ending one.

Maye was knocked out of Sunday's loss against the Detroit Lions in Munich following the team's first offensive drive. Maye, who was sacked to end the drive, appeared to sustain an ankle injury on the play. When the Patriots took the field for their second offensive drive of the day, Maye went to the locker room.

He was replaced by backup Tommy DeVito, who finished 12-for-24 passing for 81 yards, 1 touchdown and 1 interception. He was sacked four times in Detroit's 35-10 victory.

The Patriots (5-5) quickly ruled Maye as questionable to return due to an ankle injury. He was eventually ruled out for the contest.

New England has a bye next week before facing the Los Angeles Chargers on Nov. 29.

Maye wasn't the only player injured on the play. Detroit Lions star Aidan Hutchinson, who sacked Maye on the play, sustained a knee injury after celebrating the sack. He walked off the field under his own power and visited the blue medical tent on the sideline before walking to the locker room. Hutchinson was later ruled out.

#2783. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2781. Drake Maye out indefinitely with potential ankle fracture

The New England Patriots fear that Drake Maye suffered an ankle fracture during Sunday's loss to the Detroit Lions. According to a report by ESPN's Adam Schefter, he underwent further testing in Germany to determine whether or not surgery would be necessary.

Regardless of that decision, Maye will be out 'indefinitely' and potentially for the remainder of the season.

The New England Patriots appear to have suffered a major blow on Sunday. Quarterback Drake Maye, who exited the game against the Detroit Lions in the first quarter, is expected to be "out an extended period of time" and might even miss the remainder of the season altogether.

According to a report by Ian Rapoport of NFL Network, Maye suffered a "significant lower leg injury" in the Patriots' 35-10 loss to the Lions.

Maye, 24, exited the game after the opening drive. He had completed three of his first four pass attempts for 48 total yards and also gained 11 yards on a scramble, but was taken down for a third-down sack to end the series. After the play, which also saw Lions edge Aidan Hutchinson suffer a potentially season-ending injury while celebrating, the Patriots' third-year QB limped of the field.

He later entered the blue medical tent on the sidelines and after emerging made his way to the locker room. Maye was initially announced as questionable to return with an ankle injury, but was ruled out for the remainder of the game shortly thereafter.

In his absence, backup quarterback Tommy DeVito entered the contest. DeVito completed 12 of 24 passes for 81 yards with one touchdown and one interception. New England scored once with him under center.

The Patriots flew back to the United States on Sunday, after their game against the Lions in Munich. Maye is expected to undergo further testing to determine the exact severity of the injury and establish a recovery timeline.

The Patriots are on their bye this week before a Week 12 game against the Los Angeles Chargers.

#2779. Barlow ‘making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers

Barlow 'making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow decided to finish his post-practice workout Saturday with a windmill dunk.

While Barlow remains out with a "lower back stress reaction" listing on the Sixers' injury report, it's one sign that he's trending up in his rehab process.

"He's making progress," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He was a part of the practice today. It's kind of a game day type of practice … so it wasn't super intense, as you might expect. But he was out there doing some stuff. He's progressing. Just don't know when he'll be ready to go, but at least he's making some progress now."

Barlow was one of the Sixers' top role players last season, starting 59 games and contributing in a variety of areas. He averaged 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals in 23.8 minutes per game.

"Size, athleticism, rebounding," Dean Wade said of the fifth-year forward. "He's another guy that can switch one through five."

Whenever the 6-foot-9 Barlow is available for his season debut, the Sixers will welcome him back as another option at center.

With Ariel Hukporti's preseason Achilles tendon tear and Adem Bona's cervical spine sprain, the team is light on healthy resources at the five. And Joel Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management) will sit Sunday afternoon vs. the Pacers on the first leg of a back-to-back. He'll presumably return to the lineup when the Sixers visit the Nets on Monday night.

"I think (Barlow) is probably going to have to play more five than we envisioned," Nurse said, "but Jabari (Walker) is doing that as well. If the situation calls for that, I imagine that's where he'll help the most."

With the Delaware Blue Coats hosting the Maine Red Claws on Friday and Saturday, the Sixers saw an opportunity for two players outside of the regular rotation to log minutes with their G League affiliate.

Rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. suited up Friday and third-year wing Justin Edwards played both games. Philon posted 20 points, nine rebounds and eight assists in a blowout loss. Edwards shot just 1 for 10 from the floor in the first game, but he scored 23 points on 9-for-17 shooting in Saturday's win.

Nurse has largely kept a tight rotation early in the season and the 10-3 Sixers haven't had many lopsided games with ample garbage time. As of Saturday night, the Sixers ranked 29th in the NBA in bench minutes per game.

"My first belief is it's hard to get better unless you're playing," Nurse said. "That kind of trumps everything, in my opinion. Just historically, we've had a number of guys that have gone down to those games, respected it … and it's really helped them in their careers. … It's been a minute for these guys — both of them — since they've had any real court time.

"So that fit into the schedule well. … I think it will also help them to just find some rhythm, find their legs a little bit. We're going to need everybody at some point, so keep 'em going."

VJ Edgecombe was among the crowd Friday night at Chase Fieldhouse.

"They're my brothers, man," he said. "Labaron, Justin, (Caleb Love), (Rayan Rupert). I just wanted to show love. … I didn't have (anything) to do yesterday. … We have that relationship off the court, too. I'm just being supportive and showing them that I care about them."

#2777. 5 takeaways from Bills win over Jets

The Buffalo Bills beat the Jets 31-12 in Week 10 on inside MetLife Stadium, in a game that went from defensive slugfest in the first two quarters to blowout in the second half.

At 8-1, the Bills are out to their best start since 1991.

These are my takeaways from Buffalo's victory.

During the Bills' four-game win streak entering this game, which included dominance after the bye week, it felt natural to narrow focus on Buffalo's entire operation, checking for any component of the team that could improve.

And in wins over the Rams, Raiders, Ravens, and Vikings, Buffalo seemingly lacked efficiency from the pass game in a classic sense. You know, Josh Allen standing in the pocket and completing the football to pass catchers within the intended design of the play. It hadn't happened at the frequency one would expect in games in which the Bills scored 37, 33, 36, and 23 points (against the No. 1 defense in football on the road).

But in the second half against the Jets, the Bills demonstrated their ability to move the ball through the air in a normal, rhythmic way… at all levels of the field. And there was an explosive element that's been mostly missing over the past four outings.

For as good as Allen has been creating this season, and for as reliable as Buffalo has been on the ground, this team needs to be able to win with classic dropback passing too, and in the third and fourth quarters against the Jets, the Bills did just that.

The turf at MetLife Stadium has one of the most notorious reputations in the NFL. And a handful of Bills were dinged with injuries in this game, unsurprisingly.

Here's to hoping the likes of Christian Benford, Dorian Williams, and Dee Alford are OK. In their absence, Buffalo's defensive depth was immediately tested. And it shined.

The Bills defense didn't skip a beat with Kaleb Elarms-Orr, Jordan Hancock, and Maxwell Hairston on the field. But it wasn't just that trio making relief appearances for those sidelined in-game with injury.

Rookie T.J. Parker played the second-most snaps of his NFL career to date (38) and had five pressures with two sacks on 26 pass-rush snaps. Javon Solomon chipped in 12 snaps and registered a pair of pressures in his two pass-rush opportunities.

As we've come to know during the Allen era, depth can be vital in the playoffs, and Buffalo's backups are not only getting a decent amount of run this regular season but they're playing respectable football.

Brown was listed as doubtful going into this game. Ankle injury. He didn't practice Thursday or Friday. Then he told reporters while warming up pre-game he was going to play.

And I don't think anyone would've blamed him if he struggled against surging rookie, No. 2 overall pick David Bailey, on the edge. But that didn't happen.

Per Next Gen Stats, Brown squared off with Bailey 13 times and didn't allow a single pressure of Allen.

He was also the blocker attached to star veteran linebacker DeMario Davis 10 yards downfield on James Cook's 74-yard touchdown run that put the game away in the fourth quarter.

It was such a gutsy performance from Brown, who is still getting better in Year 7 at right tackle. With his height, agility, explosiveness, balance, and power, he's one of the most unique players at his position in football.

And this game was the latest piece of evidence that Brown is incredibly tough too.

So maybe not a takeaway just from this game, but I was reminded of just how tremendous of a football player Cook has become in his 157-yard performance in Week 10 that was capped with the long touchdown sprint.

Cook isn't Bijan Robinson or Derrick Henry. He's not regularly jump-cutting the opponent's entire linebacker group or running it over.

He's incredibly flexible in his lower half with outstanding vision as he works through blocks from his offensive line and one of the most dynamic open-field running backs in the game.

Only Jahmyr Gibbs has more 10-plus yard runs (34) than Cook (27). And Gibbs is the only back in football with more 15-plus yard runs (16) than the Bills feature back (14). Cook is up to 971 yards on the ground this season at 5.2 yards per. He can wear down a defense three or four yards at at time — like vs. Vikings — on 31 carries, or hit explosive plays on the ground like he did against the Jets in this one.

Through the first month of the 2026 campaign, this specific subheading would've been insane. But it's a testament to how things can change for a team — and a position group — throughout a long season in the NFL.

Terrel Bernard is 27 years old. Prime of his career. Dorian Williams is playing for a second contract. Elarms-Orr looks as ready to go in Year 1 as a team can expect from a fourth-round pick at linebacker.

In this contest, that trio had 19 tackles, two tackles for loss, three total pressures, and a sack without any missed tackles. And it aligned with how the those three have played over the past month or so and certainly since the bye week.

And Bernard, Williams, and KEO aren't simply thriving downhill. In this game, they allowed five catches for 30 yards in their coverage area. And that aligns with how well the group has collectively covered this season.

#2772. Travis Hunter now has two games with a catch and an interception. Deion Sanders had nine.

Jaguars cornerback Travis Hunter intercepted a pass on Sunday against the Titans, and doing his part-time job as a wide receiver, he also caught two passes. It was the second time Hunter has both caught and intercepted a pass in the same game.

That's a rare feat in the modern NFL, where few players play both ways. It's been four years since any player other than Hunter had both a catch and an interception in the same game. The last player to do it was New England's Marcus Jones, who did it twice in 2022.

Since the 1970 AFL-NFL merger, the player who has had both a catch and an interception in the same game the most times is Hunter's college coach, Deion Sanders, who did it nine times. Sanders did it three times while playing for the Falcons and six times while playing for the Cowboys.

Hunter is primarily a defensive player: He played 56 defensive snaps and only six offensive snaps on Sunday, and that's been typical for him this season. Overall in 2026, Hunter has played 84 percent of the Jaguars' defensive snaps and 9 percent of the Jaguars' offensive snaps. But the Jaguars say they view Hunter as a two-way player for the long term, so there's a good chance that he'll have more games with both a catch and an interception, and perhaps eclipse Sanders' modern-era record of nine such games.

But Hunter is unlikely to break the all-time record of Packers Hall of Famer Don Hutson, who had 21 games with both a catch and an interception between 1940 and 1945. Hutson had some statistics that don't look possible, such as the game when he outscored the Chicago Cardinals all by himself with six catches for 86 yards and two touchdowns, plus two interceptions for 84 return yards and a pick-six, in a 35-14 Packers victory. Hutson was by far the best receiver of his era and one of the best defensive players of his era, and he was also a pretty good kicker by the standards of the 1940s. There will never be another Don Hutson.

What Hunter is doing in 2026, however, is a special accomplishment that also deserves celebration. There's no one else playing the game in quite the same way Hunter is playing it.
